One important aspect of residential privacy is 
protection of the unwilling listener. Although in many 
locations, we expect individuals simply to avoid speech 
they do not want to hear. . . , the home is different. That 
we are captives outside the sanctuary of the home and 
subject to objectionable speech . . . does not mean we 
must be captives everywhere.... Thus, we have 
repeatedly held that individual’s are not required to 
welcome unwanted speech into their homes and that the 
government may protect this freedom. 


Frisby v. Schultz, 487 U.S. 474, 484 (1988) (internal citations and 
quotation marks omitted); see also Hynes, 425 U.S. at 616-17. Several 
of our sister circuits have also held that the state’s interest in 
protecting its citizens from undue annoyance is sufficient to support 
statutes facing intermediate scrutiny. See, e.g., Van Bergen v. 
Minnesota, 59 F.3d 1541, 1554 (8th Cir. 1995) (finding that 
Minnesota’s interest in protecting its citizens from unwanted 
annoyances was a sufficient interest to regulate automated telephone 
calls); Pennsylvania Alliance for Jobs & Energy, 743 F.2d at 187. 


83a 


Appendix G 


such fraud is insufficient to establish a real anticipated harm; 
they would instead require that Stratton itself have had 
problems with fraud before it could promulgate an ordinance 
to prevent fraud. That argument ignores the fact that the 
interest the ordinance promotes is preventing an anticipated 
harm. If we required the Village to wait until it had trouble 
with door-to-door fraud, we would be depriving it of a police 
power the Supreme Court has made clear municipalities 
possess. It also ignores the principle reaffirmed by the 
Supreme Court’s holding in City of Erie v. Pap's A.M., 529 
U.S. 277, 298 (2000), that a city may use evidence from the 
experiences of other cities that are similarly situated to 
establish a reasonable ground to believe that an anticipated 
harm is real. See also City of Renton v. Playtime Theatres, 
Inc., 475 U.S. 41, 51-53 (1986). As municipalities may take 
the experiences of other municipalities into account, we pay 
deference to the Village’s predictive judgment and the district 
court’s finding in substance that “there [was] a reasonable 
ground to fear that serious evil [would] result if free speech 
[were] practiced” without the ordinance. National Treasury 
Employees Union, 513 U.S. at 475. Accordingly, the Village 
was entitled to promulgate an ordinance that was narrowly 
tailored to promote its interests in preventing fraud and in 
protecting its residents from unwanted annoyance. 


And from our review of the record, we believe that is 
what the Village did. The ordinance promotes the Village’s 
interest in protecting the privacy of its residents by creating 
additional deterrents for canvassers, including Jehovah's 
Witnesses, who are considering ignoring a resident’s wishes 
and canvassing the resident’s home. A Jehovah's Witness is 
more likely to respect the resident’s wishes not to be 


84a 
Appendix G 


canvassed when a criminal penalty — albeit a light penalty 
— is connected to such conduct in addition to the threat of 
civil action. Thus, the penalty attached to canvassing the 
house of a resident with a No Solicitation Sign more 
effectively promotes the Village’s interest. The ordinance’s 
registration requirements also likely deter Jehovah’s 
Witnesses from canvassing homes with No Solicitation Signs 
and forms because they are aware that the Village now has 
information — name, address, organization or cause — 
helpful in apprehending someone who ignores a resident’s 
wishes. The extra deterrence the ordinance creates promotes 
the Village’s interest in protecting its residents from undue 
annoyance and that interest “would be achieved less 
effectively absent the [ordinance].” Ward, 491 U.S. at 799. 


As to the latter method of furthering the interest — the 
registration requirement — the dissent misstates the issue. It 
is not whether the requirement deters by increasing the degree 
of punishment. Rather it is whether the requirement increases 
the likelihood of punishment. It does. 


The Village’s interest in preventing fraud would also be 
achieved less effectively absent the ordinance. Absent a 
registration requirement, the Village has no way of assessing 
whether canvassers are in fact affiliated with an organization 
such as Jehovah’s Witnesses or are instead perpetrators of 
fraud using a Jehovah’s Witnesses claim as cover. Requiring 
the individuals to identify themselves as having a relationship 
with the organization provides the Village with information 
helpful in making this assessment. Consequently, the 
ordinance better equips the Village in its attempt to turn away 
perpetrators posing as Jehovah’s Witnesses. Moreover, the 


85a 


Appendix G 


requirements go further than anti-fraud laws in deterring such 
individuals from committing fraud because they know the 
Village has information that would make it easier to 


apprehend them were they to do so. 


We believe the dissent misunderstands our reasoning and 
the effect of the registration requirement. Its argument that 
the requirement burdens substantially more speech than 
necessary because it requires registration “not only for those 
wishing to engage in sales transactions — a possible avenue 
for fraud — but also for those wishing to engage in political, 
religious, or social advocacy” assumes too much. First, it 
assumes that the only avenue for engaging in fraud is posing 
as a sales person. That need not be the only avenue. Second, 
it assumes that one who intends to engage in fraud by posing 
as a sales person would be honest enough to inform the 
Village that he was intending to pose as a sales person and 
thus would register as such. That is not necessarily the case. 
It is also possible that the criminal would inform the Village 
that he was going to engage in political, religious, or social 
advocacy in order to avoid the registration requirement. 
It would be nice if the Village had the ability to discern in 
advance who, or what type of groups, will commit fraud. 
However, it does not. And absent such ability, the Village 
does not burden substantially more speech than necessary 
by requiring all individuals seeking to go door-to-door to 
register. 


Moving to the final requirement of intermediate scrutiny, 
we believe that the ordinance leaves open ample alternatives 
of communication. Indeed, the ordinance does not foreclose 
the option of going door-to-door; one only need register first. 


86a 
Appendix G 


Plaintiffs’ argument that the ordinance effectively forecloses 
that option to them because of their religious convictions, as 
we will discuss below, is foreclosed by the Court’s decision 
in Employment Division v. Smith.'° Further, there are several 
alternatives to door-to-door canvassing: Jehovah’s Witnesses 
may spread their message at stores, on street corners, in 
restaurants, in parks, and other public forums. 


This is not to say, as the dissent suggests we are holding, 
that meeting the ample alternatives requirement alone is 
sufficient to pass constitutional muster. Indeed, if that were 
the case, we would have foregone the rest of the analysis, 
evaluated the alternatives available to Jehovah’s Witnesses, 
and called it a day. 


As the ordinance satisfies the ample alternatives as well 
as the other requirements of intermediate scrutiny, we hold 
that the ordinance does not violate plaintiffs’ free speech 
rights. 


B. Free Exercise Claims 
1. Whether the Ordinance is Unconstitutional 


We reach the same conclusion with respect to plaintiffs’ 
free exercise challenges. Other than arguments that the 
ordinance fails strict scrutiny, plaintiffs offer no arguments 
for why the ordinance violates their free exercise rights. 


10. Plaintiffs and dissent argue that the Village could have 
included in the ordinance an exception from its requirements for 
Jehovah's Witnesses. If the Village had included such an exception, 
however, the ordinance would not be content neutral. Therefore, we 
do not believe that to be a viable alternative here. 


87a 


Appendix G 


Instead, they cite a handful of Supreme Court cases all 
of which, they claim, stand for the proposition that the 
“Supreme Court has continuously held that Jehovah’s 
Witnesses cannot be required to obtain a permit in order to 
engage in door-to-door religious speech.” And that “[i]n not 
one [of those cases} did the Supreme Court hold that 
Jehovah’s Witnesses must obtain a permit prior to engaging 
in door-to-door dissemination of Bible-based ideas or 
literature.” That is probably because few of those cases 
involved an ordinance requiring such a permit. Instead, many 
of the ordinances flatly prohibited the dissemination of ideas 
either in a public forum or door-to-door. See Marsh vy. 
Alabama, 326 U.S. 501, 504 (1946); Martin, 319 U.S. at 
142; Schneider v. New Jersey, 308 U.S. 147, 154, 156 (1939). 
The ordinances that did require a permit, unlike the Village’s 
ordinance, left the decision whether to grant the permit at 
the discretion of a municipal officer. See Tucker v. Texas, 
326 U.S. 517, 519 (1946); Cantwell, 310 U.S. at 302; 
Schneider, 308 U.S. at 158; Lovell, 303 U.S. at 451. In short, 
contrary to plaintiffs’ assertions, not one of these cases 
mandates a finding that the ordinance violates their free 
exercise rights. 


Nor does a general review of the ordinance under current 
law warrant such a finding. Under recent Supreme Court 
precedent, a law that is content neutral and of general 
applicability does not violate an individual’s free exercise 
rights. See Smith, 494 U.S. at 879; Church of Lukumi Babalu 
Aye, Inc. v. City of Hialeah, 508 U.S. 520, 531-32 (1993). 
One that is not content neutral or not of general applicability, 
on the other hand, must pass strict judicial scrutiny. 
See Church of Lukumi Babalu Aye, 508 U.S. at 531-32. 


88a 
Appendix G 


We find the law to be both content neutral and of general 
applicability. In the context of free exercise challenges, a 
law is not content neutral if “the object of the law is to 
infringe upon or restrict religious practices because of their 
religious motivation.” Jd. at 532. To determine whether the 
law has such an object, we look to the language of the law; 
the operation of the law; and how officials apply the law. 
See id. at 532-35. A law is facially content based if it refers 
to a religious practice without a discernable secular meaning. 
See id. at 532. The ordinance does not. While the 
No Solicitation Form did refer specifically to Jehovah’s 
Witnesses, it is not part of the text of the ordinance. Further, 
the district court ordered the Village to remove that language. 
The ordinance is also neutral in its operation; it imposes the 
same burdens on all individuals or organizations seeking to 
canvass door-to-door. See id. at 535. Finally, we believe that 
the Village officials have applied the ordinance neutrally. 
There is no indication that the Mayor discriminated against 
religious organizations in determining whether to grant 
permits. Indeed, there is little room for him to make any 
decision once the applicant completes the Registration Form. 
And contrary to plaintiffs’ assertion, the Mayor’s statements 
that he would not grant Jehovah’s Witnesses an exemption 
from the ordinance’s time restrictions on canvassing does 
not compel a finding that he unequally applied the ordinance. 
He has never applied the ordinance’s time restrictions to them 
because they have never requested an exemption from the 
restrictions. 


We also find that the law is of general applicability. To 
be generally applicable, the law must not be the product of a 
government action that, in pursuit of legitimate interests, 


89a 


Appendix G 


“impose[s] burdens only on conduct motivated by religious 
belief essential to the protection of the rights guaranteed by 
the Free Exercise Clause.” /d. at 543. The ordinance is not 
the product of such action. The Village has made clear that 
its interest was in preventing fraud and protecting its residents 
from undue annoyance. In pursuing these ends, the ordinance 
burdens any individual seeking to canvass door-to-door. As 
the ordinance is content neutral and generally applicable, we 
hold that it does not violate plaintiffs’ free exercise rights. 


2. Exemption from the Ordinance 


For the same reason, we deny plaintiffs’ request for an 
exemption from the requirements of the ordinance. Plaintiffs 
argue that they should be granted an exemption from the 
ordinance because seeking permission to spread their 
religious beliefs violates their religious convictions. To 
support their argument they cite Wisconsin v. Yoder, 406 U.S. 
205 (1972), for the proposition that where an individual’s 
beliefs are a matter of deep rooted religious convictions and 
complying with the regulation would violate those 
convictions, the individual should be granted an exemption 
from complying with the reguiation. 


That is not the law. As the Court noted in Smith, it “has 
never held that an individual’s religious beliefs excuse him 
from compliance with an otherwise valid law prohibiting 
conduct that the State is free to regulate.” Smith, 494 U.S. at 
878-79. Our review indicates that the ordinance is valid, and 
therefore, plaintiffs must comply with it. 


In conclusion, we affirm the district court’s judgment 
that the ordinance does not violate plaintiffs’ First 


90a 


Appendix G 


Amendment rights and we deny them an exemption from 
complying with the ordinance. We now review the Village’s 
challenges to the district court’s judgment awarding plaintiffs 
attorneys’ fees. 


Ill. ATTORNEYS’ FEES 


Briefly, the Village argues that the district court erred in 
awarding attorneys’ fees by concluding (1) that plaintiffs were 
the prevailing party and (2) that they obtained substantial 
relief and were thus entitled to compensation for the time 
their attorneys expended on the entire course of the litigation. 
It contends that plaintiffs’ relief was so small — and not of 
the type sought — that they cannot be considered the 
prevailing party. And, even if they are, by no means did they 
obtain the relief for which they hoped — being exempted 
from complying with the ordinance — and therefore cannot 
be considered to have achieved substantial success. 


In reviewing an award of attorneys’ fees under § 1988, 
we review the factual findings underlying the district court’s 
determination of prevailing status for clear error and its award 
of attorneys’ fees for abuse of discretion. See Gregory v. 
Shelby County, 220 F.3d 433, 446 (6th Cir. 2000). “A district 
court abuses its discretion when it relies on clearly erroneous 
findings of facts” or “when it improperly applies the law or 
uses an erroneous legal standard.” Owner-Operator Indep. 
Drivers Ass'n, Inc. v. Bissell, 210 F.3d 595, 597 (6th Cir. 
2000). 


We have no trouble agreeing with the district court’s 
conclusion that plaintiffs were in fact the prevailing party. 


9la 


Appendix G 


As a result of the litigation, their legal relationship with the 
Village was altered in a manner which they sought. 
See Hensley v. Eckerhart, 461 U.S. 424, 433 (1983). The 
question of whether this success is sufficient to be considered 
substantial is much tougher. The Village is correct that 
plaintiffs must still register prior to canvassing, and 
eliminating that requirement was a focal point of their 
lawsuit. As it is a close call, we defer to the factual findings 
of the district court and conclude that it did not abuse its 
discretion in awarding attorneys’ fees in the amount of 
$39,340.87; $15,464.04 in out of pocket expenses; and 
$4,087.50 for paralegal costs. 


Therefore, we affirm the district court’s judgment 
awarding plaintiffs attorneys’ fees and costs. 


IV. CONCLUSION 


For the foregoing reasons, we affirm the district court’s 
judgments. 


92a 
Appendix G 
CONCURRING IN PART, DISSENTING IN PART 


RONALD LEE GILMAN, Circuit Judge, concurring in 
part and dissenting in part. I agree with the majority that the 
Village of Stratton’s ordinance is not unconstitutionally vague 
and does not violate the free exercise rights of the Jehovah’s 
Witnesses. Because the ordinance is content neutral on its 
face, I also agree that intermediate scrutiny is the proper 
standard applicable to its review. Finally, | agree with the 
majority’s decision to affirm the award of attorneys fees to 
the Jehovah’s Witnesses. 


I disagree, however, with the majority’s application of 
the intermcdiate scrutiny standard, and would hold that the 
ordinance violates the First Amendment by burdening 
substantially more speech than is necessary to further the 
Village’s legitimate interests. See Ward v. Rock Against 
Racism, 491 U.S. 781, 799 (1989). Consequently, I would 
declare that the ordinance’s permit requirement is 
unconstitutional as applied to the Jehovah’s Witnesses. 


The Supreme Court has long since recognized that the 
regulation of door-to-door solicitors must comport with the 
First Amendment. See Cantwell v. Connecticut, 310 U.S. 296 
(1940) (striking down an ordinance specifically requiring 
door-to-door solicitors for religious, charitable, or 
philanthropic causes to first obtain a permit). Content-neutral 
regulations, on the other hand, are analyzed under an 
intermediate scrutiny standard of review. See Ward, 491 U.S. 
at 798-99 (applying intermediate scrutiny to content-neutral 
noise regulations). This standard, however, does not mean 
that a content-neutral regulation “may burden substantially 


93a 


Appendix G 


more speech than is necessary to further the government’s 
legitimate interests.” Jd. at 799. Rather, a content-neutral 
regulation must “focus[ ] on the source of the evils the 
[government] seeks to eliminate” and “eliminate them 
without at the same time banning or significantly restricting 
a substantial quantity of speech that does not create the same 
evils.” Jd. at 800 n.7. 


In the present case, the Village has articulated a legitimate 
interest in eliminating two evils — fraud and unwanted 
annoyance. The question, then, is whether the means chosen 
by the Village to implement 1ts goals significantly restricts a 
substantial quantity of speech that does not create the same 
evils. I am of the opinion that the ordinance does just that. 


First, by subjecting noncommercial solicitation to its 
permit requirements, the ordinance restricts a substantial 
amount of speech unrelated to the prevention of fraud. The 
ordinance requires a permit not only for those wishing to 
engage in sales transactions — a possible avenue for fraud 
— but also for those wishing to engage in political, religious, 
or social advocacy. Indeed, the Village’s own “No Solicitation 
Registration Form” acknowledges that the ordinance reaches 
political candidates, Christmas carolers, and campaigners for 
social issues, none of whom create the threat of fraud that 
the Village intended to address. The ordinance therefore 
burdens substantially more speech than is necessary to 
promote the legitimate government interest of fraud protection. 
See Cleveland Area Bd. of Realtors v. City of Euclid, 
88 F.3d 383, 386-90 (6th Cir. 1996) (applying an intermediate 
scrutiny standard to declare an ordinance unconstitutional 
that banned all yard signs, concluding that the ordinance 


94a 


Appendix G 


burdened substantially more speech than necessary to 
promote the legitimate government interest in aesthetics). 


The majority emphasizes that “the ordinance does not 
foreclose the option of going door-to-door; one only need 
register first.” Majority Op. at 20-21. Such an argument 
implies that because a person can engage in speech once he 
or she has obtained a permit, all permit requirements are 
constitutional. This is clearly not the law. See Cantwell, 310 
U.S. at 303-07. Although a permit requirement is not per se 
invalid, it bears a heavy burden to pass constitutional muster 
because it gives public officials the power to deny the use of 
a forum in advance of actual expression. See Community for 
Creative Non-Violence v. Turner, 893 F.2d 1387, 1390 (D.C. 
Cir. 1990) (finding unconstitutional, on the basis of 
intermediate scrutiny, an ordinance requiring permits of all 
people wishing to engage in content-neutral, noncommercial 
speech on transit authority property, where the “[p]ersons 
desiring to engage in any organized free speech activities in 
the ... forum are subject to the permit requirement”). 
Consequently, the type of persons and entities subject to the 
permit requirement must be analyzed to determine an 
ordinance’s impact on speech. See id. at 1393. 


The majority also states that the Jehovah’s Witnesses 
“may spread their message at stores, on street corners, in 
restaurants, in parks, and other public forums.” Majority Op. 
at 21. The Supreme Court, however, has stressed the unique 
role of door-to-door solicitation as a means of communication, 
stating that “as every person acquainted with political life 
knows, door to door campaigning is one of the most accepted 
techniques of seeking political support .... Door to door 


95a 


Appendix G 


distribution of circulars is essential to the poorly financed 
causes of little people.” Martin v. City of Struthers, 319 U.S. 
141, 146 (1943). I believe that this reasoning applies with 
equal force to the communication of religious beliefs. The 
availability, therefore, of other outlets fox such speech does 
not alleviate the special burden that this ordnance places on 
door-to-door solicitation. 


The narrowly tailored requirement demands a balance 
between the legitimate interests of the government and the 
constitutional rights of individuals. However, the majority 
opinion concludes in a very generalized way that the Village 
of Stratton’s registration requirement is constitutional 
because it “more effectively promotes the Village’s interest.” 
Majority Op. at 19. But just as the majority accuses the dissent 
of assuming that those who intend to commit fraud would 
register as salespeople, the majority assumes (in my view, 
incorrectly) that those intending to commit fraud would in 
fact register at all. 


As to the Village’s asserted interest in protecting 
homeowners from “unwanted annoyances,” this interest can 
be achieved by less restrictive means, such as by enforcing 
laws against trespass. See Discovery Network, Inc. v. City of 
Cincinnati, 946 F.2d 464, 473 n.14 (6th Cir. 1991) (finding 
unconstitutional, even under intermediate scrutiny, a Cincinnati 
ordinance prohibiting the distribution of commercial 
handbills on public property because “there are many options 
available to the city that would address its aesthetic, safety, 
and proliferation concerns without placing the significant 
burden on commercial speech that the ordinance does”). 


96a 


Appendix G 


Indeed, the Village already effectively achieves its interest 
in protecting homeowners from unwanted annoyances 
through the section of the ordinance that forbids solicitation 
at residences where the homeowners have posted “No 
Solicitation” signs. The same penalty, a fourth-degree 
misdemeanor, is imposed for violating the “No Solicitation” 
section as for violating the registration section of the 
ordinance. Thus someone who would register because they 
are deterred by the penalty for failing to do so would 
presumably already be deterred from ignoring a “No 
Solicitation” sign, while someone who is willing to risk the 
penalty imposed for ignoring a “No Solicitation” sign would 
presumably also be willing to risk the penalty for failing to 
register. The registration requirement, accordingly, is not 
narrowly tailored to protect homeowners from unwanted 
annoyances. See Ward, 491 U.S. at 799 (stating that a 
regulation is narrowly tailored under the intermediate scrutiny 
standard if it “promotes a substantial government interest 
that would be achieved less effectively absent the 
regulation”). 


“Our cases make clear that in assessing the 
reasonableness of a regulation [that affects speech], we must 
weigh heavily the fact that communication is involved.” 
Grayned v. City of Rockford, 408 U.S. 104, 116 (1972). 
Consequently, “[w]hen the Government restricts speech, the 
Government bears the burden of proving the constitutionality 
of its actions.” United States v. Playboy Entm t Group, Inc., 
120 S. Ct. 1878, 1888 (2000). This burden, however, “is not 
satisfied by mere speculation or conjecture; rather, a 
governmental body seeking to sustain a restriction on 
commercial speech must demonstrate that the harms it recites 


97a 


Appendix G 


are real and that its restriction will in fact alleviate them to a 
material degree.” Florida Bar v. Went for It, Inc., 515 U.S. 
618, 625 (1995) (applying the intermediate scrutiny standard 
to restrictions on legal advertising) (citations omitted and 
emphasis added). In this case, the Village of Stratton has 
failed to demonstrate either the reality of the harm or the 
efficacy of the restriction. 


For all of the reasons set forth above, | would hold that 
the ordinance’s permit requirement is an unconstitutional 
infringement on the Jehovah's Witnesses’ First Amendment 


rights.

ATTENTION: RICHARD D. MOAKE, 
ASSOCIATE GENERAL COUNSEL 


RE: CHAPTER 116, CODIFIED ORDINANCES OF 
THE VILLAGE OF STRATTON, OHIO AND 
YOUR LETTER OF APRIL 10, 1998 


129a 


Appendix 1(5) 


Dear Mr. Moake: 


Your April 10, 1998 letter, addressed to the Honorable 
John M. Abdalla, Mayor of the Village of Stratton, Ohio, has 
been referred to me for response. 


With respect to the points raised in your letter, please be 
advised as follows: 


1) Definition of “Canvassers”, “Solicitors”, 
and other classes described in Section 116.01. 
I believe that each of the terms included in the 
phrase, “canvassers, solicitors, peddlers, hawkers, 
itinerant merchants and transient vendors of 
merchandise and services,” has a plain and ordinary 
meaning, which obviates the need for further specific 
definition in the Ordinance, itself. 


However, if you have a suggested definition, 
please submit each definition to me, so that I can 
evaluate the question of whether the Ordinance 
should be amended to adopt a specific definition for 
‘each class identified in that section. 


The Ordinance does not preclude “soliciting the 
attention of . . .” any person, and it does not prohibit, 
“explaining any ... organization or cause,”; but 
rather, it prohibits the uninvited entry onto private 
property, for those purposes and other purposes. 


The gravamen of the Ordinance is a prohibition 
against uninvited entry — i.e., entry in the nature of 
a trespass. 


130a 
Appendix 1(5) 


2) Speech About A “Cause”. The Ordinance 
does not prohibit (nor does it purport to prohibit) 
speech. It prohibits uninvited entry, and it regulates 
invited door-to-door canvassing, soliciting, 
peddling, hawking, etc. 


3) Invited Entry. In your letter, you raise a third 
point, in which you seem to speculate on the Mayor's 
interpretation of the Ordinance. You say that, under 
the Mayor’s interpretation, a Jehovah’s Witness 
would be prohibited from going into a residence for 
the purpose of talking about the Bible, even if he 
has been invited, unless he obtains a $10.00 permit 
to “visit that one (1) residence.” I have no reason to 
believe that the Mayor interprets the Ordinance that 
way, and I do not interpret the Ordinance that way. 
The Ordinance addresses invited visits by 
canvassers, solicitors, peddlers, hawkers, itinerant 
merchants and transient vendors of merchandise or 
services. It is obvious that the Ordinance has no 
application, at all, to an invited guest, who wishes 
to talk about any subject whatsoever. 


If you believe that the Ordinance is not clearly 
limited to door-to-door canvassing, soliciting, etc., 
feel free to suggest any language which, in your 
opinion, makes that limitation clear. Then, I will give 
the Village Council an opportunity to consider the 
question of whether it will amend the Ordinance. 


In the meantime, I see no evidence showing that 
the Ordinance has been applied to an invited guest 


l3la 
Appendix I(5) 


who accepts an invitation to visit a single residence, 
for any purpose whatsoever. 


You correctly point out that the purpose of the 
Ordinance is to protect against a “risk of unwanted 
intrusion (and) annoyance” to Village residents. 
Again, if you have suggested language, which, in 
your opinion, better reflects that purpose, please 
submit it for the Village Council’s consideration. 


In the meantime, I know of no application of 
the Ordinance, where the Ordinance has been applied 
to a person who has accepted an invitation to another 
person’s home. 


4. Emergency Clause. | disagree with your 
view of the emergency clause for two (2) reasons. 
First, courts do not substitute their judgment for that 
of the legislative authority, with respect to the 
declaration of reasons in an emergency clause of a 
municipal ordinance. Second, I see a clear 
relationship between the stated purpose of the 

' Ordinance and the activities of canvassers, solicitors, 
etc. who fail to identify themselves and engage in 
unwanted intrusions at times and in manners which 
cause annoyance, etc. to the ordinary citizen who is 
less able to identify and regulate the conduct of such 
persons. I do not presume to know or foresee the 
conduct of specific Jehovah’s Witnesses in the 
future. I have no way of knowing whether each and 
every one of them goes peacefully from door to door 
talking about events and ideas (as you seem to 


132a 
Appendix I(5) 


presume in the fourth issue raised in your letter). 
However, it is clear to me that the Ordinance does 
not target the Jehovah’s Witnesses or any religious 
group, and it does not target speech. Its clear intent, 
purpose and application is in connection with 
unauthorized entry onto private property by door- 
to-door “canvassers, solicitors,”, etc. | 

5) 9:00 a.m. to 5:00 p.m. Hours. 1 do not agree 
with your fifth point. The Ordinance does not restrict 
the right of a resident and his invited guest to have 
contact and discussion during any time of the day or 
night. It affects only the “activity permitted under 
authority of this Chapter”, — i.e. door-to-door 


canvassing, soliciting, etc. by those who register and 
obtain a permit. 


| 6) Leaving the Decision to the Resident. Your 
sixth point was that the Ordinance determines the 
rights of each resident, with respect to the type of 
speech that may be conducted at his residence. I do 
not see any basis for that conclusion. Nowhere in 
the Ordinance do I see any restriction on any person’s 
expression of any idea. What I see is a limitation 
upon entering private property. 


I think the Village would be willing to work with you, in 
order to resolve the problem which you perceive. However, 
it does not seem productive to have people in town, appearing 
to be canvassing, soliciting, etc. door-to-door (and entering 
upon private property without the permission of the property 
owner), without the proper identification, registration and 


133a 
Appendix I(5) 


permit. That type of conduct seems to be motivated by the 
intent to cause confrontation, rather than solution. (Before 
such confrontation, a person may clearly appear to be 
canvassing and soliciting door-to-door, by virtue of uninvited 
entry [indeed, in some cases, trespass] in violation of the 
Ordinance, and, then, after the confrontation, they make the 
claim that they intended only to exercise their Constitutional 
Rights of Free Speech.) 


The road to a solution is cooperation. Therefore, in the 
spirit of cooperation, I suggest: 


a) Since the previous correspondence and this 
correspondence clearly explains the legislative intent 
of the Ordinance, I am inviting you to submit to me 
suggested language, modifications of language, and 
suggested amendments, which you see as appropriate 
to protecting Village inhabitants against uninvited 
entry/trespass onto their private property. I will 
consider those suggestions, and I will report back to 
you with respect to the Village’s position on each 
suggestion. 


b) In the meantime, if a member of your group 
intends to canvass, solicit, etc. in the Village, please 
let us know in advance, in order that we might avoid 
needless confrontation. 


_c) In the meantime, if a member of your group 
is invited to the home of a homeowner in the Village 
of Stratton, Ohio, that member may feel free to make 
that visit without fear of application of the 
Ordinance. 


134a 
Appendix I(5) 


I look forward to hearing from you at your earliest 
convenience. 


Thank you. 
Sincerely, 
s/ Frank J. Bruzzese

Re: Chapter 116, Codified Ordinances of the 
Village of Stratton 


Dear Mr. Bruzzese: 


~ Thank you for your letter of April 17, 1998, responding 
to my April 10, 1998, letter to Mayor Abdalla. We appreciate 
both the thoughts that went into your letter and its cooperative 
tone. 


From your letter, it seems that the Village will not apply 
sections 116.03 through 116.07 to the activities of Jehovah’s 
Witnesses. Thus, potential conflict stems only from section 
116.01 and the enforcement and penalty sections related to it. 


136a 
Appendix 1(5) 


As stated in your letter, the gravamen of section 116.01 
is a prohibition against uninvited entry. It is on this very point 
that we believe the ordinance is patently defective. In effect, 
the ordinance makes uninvited entry on a property a trespass 
without requiring the property resident to first indicate to 
potential entrants that entry is not permitted. Although I am 
sure that you have access to it, for your convenience, I enclose 
herewith a copy of the United States Supreme Court case 
entitled Martin v. City of Struthers. This case is still good 
law and has been cited affirmatively in many cases since 
1943. Under Martin, ordinances like section 116.01 are 
unconstitutional. A property resident may preclude entry on 
his property by one or more classes of people, a fact which 
may be made known either verbally or by posted notice. 
However, a municipality may not by ordinance preclude 
uninvited entry on private property. 


Because of the nature of their activity and the manner in 
which they conduct it, Jehovah’s Witnesses do not fall within 
any of the categories of persons listed in section 116.01 of 
the ordinance. Thus, even if the ordinance were 
constitutional, it would not apply to the activities of Jehovah’s 
Witnesses. 


In many communities in this country, as a courtesy to 
local law enforcement personnel, Jehovah’s Witnesses 
voluntarily give the municipality advance notice by telephone 
that they will be conducting door-to-door ministry on a 
particular date and in a particular area. As a way to resolve 
the current matter in Stratton, our local congregation would 
be willing to contact the Village Police Department by 
telephone before conducting door-to-door ministry in the 


137a 
Appendix 1(5) 


Village. The caller would state the approximate time that 
such ministry would take place, the approximate number of 
people involved, and the general location where they would 
be. In this way, your police would be aware of their activity 
on the particular day in question. If the Village would agree 
to this approach, then the issue of whether or not section 
116.01 is unconstitutional would not need to be determined. 


To indicate our good faith in the matter, until we hear 
from you, we will advise our local congregation not to 
conduct door-to-door activity in the Village and to restrict 
ministry activity to visiting residénces to which they have 
been invited. 


We look forward to hearing from you at your earliest 
convenience. 


Yours very truly, 

s/ Richard Moake 

Richard D. Moake 

Associate General Counsel

ATTENTION: RICHARD D. MOAKE,

RE: “NO SOLICITATION” ORDINANCE 
Dear Mr. Moake: 
I have continued doing legal research, in order to address 


each one of the Constitutional criticisms that you have raised, 
and, as a result of that research, | have drafted the attached 


143a 
Appendix 1(5) 


proposed Ordinance, to amend the Village’s Ordinance, in 
order to avoid an unnecessary litigation, while, at the same 
time, preserving reasonable regulations which are designed 
to prevent trespass, invasions of privacy, and solicitation by 
unidentified or illegitimate con artists. 


Please review the attached draft, to see if it satisfactorily 
addresses each of your constitutional concerns. 


The gist of the Ordinance is as follows: 


1) Section 116.01 declares that soliciting 
without a permit is declared to be a nuisance and is 
prohibited. 


2) Section 116.02 authorizes the police to 
prevent people from soliciting without a permit. 


3) Section 116.03 requires door-to-door 
solicitors to register with the Office of the Mayor, 
in order to get a permit. On the “Solicitor’s 
Registration Form”, they must identify themselves, 
their organization, their address, the nature of their 
business, the specific address of each property at 
which they intend to solicit, the length of time for 
which a permit is requested, and other informativc... 


4) Section 116.04 automatically entitles an 
applicant to a permit, if he properly fills out the 
“Solicitor’s Registration Form,” and he pays $10.00. 
(However, the permit entitles him to go only to the 
specific addresses which he listed in his “Solicitor’s 
Registration Form.”) 


144a 
Appendix 1(5) 


Section 116.04 requires the solicitor to show his 
permit, when he is requested to do so by a police 
officer or any person who he solicits. 


If a solicitor has a permit, he is exempt from 
Section 116.01. (That means that the mere fact that 
he is going door-to-door is not a nuisance, unless he 
goes to an address which he failed to list on his form, 
or unless he goes to an address which is prohibited 
under Section 116.07.) 


5) Section 116.05 restricts soliciting between 
the hours of 9:00 a.m. and 5:00 p.m. 


6) Section 116.06 authorizes the Mayor to deny 
a permit only for specific reasons. (The Mayor does 
not have any discretion which would enable 
invidious discrimination. ) 


It also authorizes the Mayor to revoke a permit 
that has previously been issued, if revocation is based 
on a specific ground. 


7) Section 116.07 authorizes the property 
owner/occupant to prohibit solicitation, even if the 
solicitor has a permit. 


It is the property owner/occupant who makes 
the choice. The property owner/occupant can file a 
“No Solicitation Registration Form” with the Office 
of the Mayor, and post a “No Solicitation” sign on 
his property. If he does so, no one (even someone 


145a 
Appendix (5) 


who holds a permit) may enter into his private 
property to solicit at his property, unless he 
specifically lists them as an exception. 


The property owner can give the Mayor a list of 
people or organizations who are allowed to solicit. 
(For example, if he doesn’t mind the Boy Scouts, 
Girl Scouts, a church organization (including the 
Jehovah’s Witnesses), or other class of solicitor, he 
can, simply, list them as an exception. [See Section 


116.07(b)(3).]) 


If the property owner does not list a person or 
organization as an exception, the property owner is 
telling the Village (on his “No Solicitation 
Registration Form”) that the solicitor is a trespasser. 


The bottom line is that the Village will not be making 
the choice as to which type of people or organizations may 
solicit. The Village will not discriminate at all. The Village 
will issue a permit to everyone who properly registers. 
However, the private citizen who owns property in the Village 
will make the choice, as to who is allowed to solicit on his 
property. In his “No Solicitation Registration Form” he can 
list any organization that he does not object to. He is telling 
the Village that he prohibits solicitation, by filing a “No 
Solicitation Registration Form” and by posting his property 
with a “No Solicitation” sign. 


Then, anyone who engages in solicitation (with or 
without a permit) is violating the Ordinance, unless the 
property owner specifically listed that solicitor as an 
exception. 


146a 
Appendix I(5) 


The Village will treat everyone alike, whereas the 
property owner gets to say who is allowed to come onto his 
property and who is not allowed. What do you think? 


Thank you. 
Sincerely, 
s/ Frank J. Bruzzese 
; FRANK J. BRUZZESE

Appendix 1(5) 


116.01 UNINVITED PEDDLING AND SOLICITING 
DECLARED A NUISANCE. 


The practice of going in and upon private property 
and/or the private residences of Village residents in the 
Village by canvassers, solicitors, peddlers, hawkers, itinerant 
merchants or transient vendors of merchandise or services, 
not having been invited to do so by the owners or occupants 
of such private property or residences, and not having first 
obtained a permit pursuant to Section 116.03 of this Chapter, 
for the purpose of advertising, promoting, selling and/or 
explaining any product, service, organization or cause, OF 
for the purpose of soliciting orders for the sale of goods, 
wares, merchandise or services, is hereby declared to be a 
nuisance and is prohibited. 


116.02 ENFORCEMENT. 


The Chief of Police (Village Marshall) and Village Police 
Officers are hereby required and authorized to abate any such 
nuisance as is described in Section 116.01. 


116.03 REGISTRATION REQUIRED — PROHIBITION 
OF UNREGISTERED SOLICITATION, ETC. 


(a) No canvasser, solicitor, peddler, hawker, itinerant 
merchant or transient vendor of merchandise or services who 
is described in Section 116.01 of this Chapter and who intends 
to go in or upon private property or a private residence in the 
Village for any of the purposes described in Section 116.01 . 
shall go in or upon such private property or residence without 
first registering in the office of the Mayor and obtaining a 
permit to do so. 


150a 
Appendix 1(5) 


(b) The registration required by subsection (a) hereof 
shall be made by filing a Solicitor’s Registration Form, at 
the office of the Mayor, on a form furnished for such purpose. 
The Form shall be completed by the Registrant and it shall 
then contain the following information: 


(1) The name and home address of the Registrant 
and Registrant’s residence for five years next 
preceding the date of registration; 


(2) A brief description of the nature and purpose 
of the business, promotion, solicitation, 


organization, cause, and/or the goods or 
services offered; 


(3) The name and address of the employer or 
affiliated organization, with credentials from 
the employer or organization showing the exact 
relationship and authority of the Applicant; 


(4) The length of time for which the privilege to 
canvass or solicit is desired; 


(5) The specific address of each private residence 
at which the Registrant intends to engage in 
the conduct described in Section 116.01 of this 
Chapter; and, 


(6) Such other information concerning the 
Registrant and its business or purpose as may 


be reasonably necessary to accurately describe 
the nature of the privilege desired. 


1S5la 
Appendix I(5) 


116.04 ISSUANCE OF PERMIT; FEE, CONTENT AND 
DISPLAY. 


Each Registrant who complies with Section 116.03(b) 
shall be furnished a permit upon the payment by, the 
Registrant of a fee of ten dollars ($10.00). The permit shall 
indicate that the applicant has registered as required by 
Section 116.03 of this Chapter. No permittee shall go in or 
upon any premises not listed on the Registrant’s Solicitor’s 
Registration Form. 


Each person shall at all times, while exercising the 
privilege in the Village incident to such permit, carry upon 
his person his permit and the same shall be exhibited by such 
person whenever he is requested to do so by any police officer 
or by any person who is solicited. 


Section 116.01 of this Chapter shall not apply and shall 
not be construed to apply to the holder of a valid permit issued 
pursuant to this Section. 


116.05 TIME LIMITATIONS. 


No activity permitted under authority of this chapter shall 
commence prior to 9:00 a.m. nor continue after 5:00 p.m. 
This time limitation shall be stated on the permit. 


116.06 DENIAL OR REVOCATION OF PERMIT. 


Permits described in Section 116.04 of this Chapter may 
be denied or revoked by the Mayor for any one or more of 
the following reasons: 


152a 
Appendix 1(5) 


(a) Incomplete information provided by the 
Registrant in the Solicitor’s Registration Form. 


(b) Fraud or misrepresentation contained in the 
Solicitor’s Registration Form. 


(c) Fraud, misrepresentation or false statements 
made in the course of conducting the activity. 


(d) Violation of any of the provisions of this 
chapter or of other Codified Ordinances or of 
any State or Federal Law. 


(e) Conducting canvassing, soliciting or business 
in such a manner as to constitute a trespass 
upon private property. 


(f) The permittee ceases to possess the qualifica- 


tions required in this chapter for the original 
registration. 


The revocation of a permit shall be in addition to any 
penalty provided in Section 116.99 of the Codified 
Ordinances of the Village of Stratton, Ohio, or any other 
penalty that may be imposed upon the permittee in accordance 
with law. 


116.07 OWNER’S/OCCUPANT’S PROHIBITION 
AGAINST ENTRY. 


(a) Notwithstanding the provisions of any other Section 
of this Chapter 116, any person, firm or corporation who is 
the owner or lawful occupant of private property within the 


153a 
Appendix 1(5) 


territorial limits of the Village of Stratton, Ohio, may prohibit 
the practice of going in or ur«a the private property and/or 
the private residence of such owuer or occupant, by uninvited 
canvassers, solicitors, peddlers, hawkers, itinerant merchants 
or transient vendors, by registering its property in accordance 
with Subdivision (b) of this Section and by posting upon 
each such registered property a sign which reads “No 
Solicitation” in a location which is reasonably visible to 


persons who intend to enter upon such property. 


(b) The registration authorized by Subsection (a) hereof 
shall be made by filing a “No Solicitation Registration Form”, 
at the office of the Mayor, on a form furnished for such 
purpose. The form shall be completed by the property owner 
or occupant and it shall then contain the following 


information: 


(1) The name and address of the owner or occupant 
who wishes to prohibit uninvited canvassing, 
soliciting, peddling, hawking, merchandising 
and/or transient vending upon the private 
property of the owner or occupant, 


(2) The specific address of each property at which 
the owner or occupant prohibits such conduct; 
and, 


(3) A written and signed statement which reads: 
“I, the undersigned, am the owner or lawful 
occupant of private property which is described 
in this No Solicitation Form, and I, hereby, give 
notice that I prohibit the practice of uninvited 


154a 
Appendix 1(5) 


canvassers, solicitors, peddlers, hawkers 
itinerant merchants or transient vendors 
coming upon my private property for the 
purpose of soliciting the attention of any 
occupant of that property or for the purpose of 
advertising, promoting, selling and/or explaining 
any product, service, organization or Cause, or 


for the purpose of soliciting orders for the sales 
of any product. 


JT have posted a sign which reads, “No 
Solicitation”, at the property, and the sign is 
located so that it is reasonably visible to 
persons who might be considering entering 
upon the property. 


I consider any person enterin 
g upon the 
property for a prohibited purpose to be a 
trespasser. 


The only exceptions to this prohibition are 
the persons and organizations listed below: 


155a 
Appendix 1(5) 


(c) No uninvited canvasser, solicitor, peddler, hawker, 
itinerant merchant or transient vendor of merchandise or 
services, shall go in or upon the private property of an owner 
or occupant of property who has registered and posted such 
property in accordance with Subsections (a) and (b) of this 
Section, for the purpose of advertising, promoting, selling 
or explaining any product, service, organization or cause, or 
for the purpose of soliciting orders for the sale of goods, 
wares, merchandise or services. 


(d) The holder of a permit issued pursuant to Section 
116.04 of this Chapter shall not be exempt from the

holder of such permit shall not go in or upon any private 
property which has been registered and posted in accordance 
with Subsection (a) and (b) of this Section, for the purpose 
of advertising, promoting, selling or explaining any product, 
service, organization or cause, or for the purpose of soliciting 
orders for the sale of goods, wares, merchandise or services.

SECTION 2: This Ordinance is hereby declared to be an 
emergency Ordinance, necessary to the preservation of the 
public peace,safety and welfare of the citizens of the Village 
of Stratton, Ohio, and such emergency exists by reason of 
the fact that the failure to regulate the time and manner of 
the conduct which is regulated by the above Ordinance, and 
the failure to identify those engaged in such conduct, creates 
a risk of unwanted intrusion, annoyance and potential harm 
to Village residents who are less able, than the Village, to 
identify and regulate the conduct of persons, corporations, 
entities and organizations engaged in such conduct. 


SECTION 3: This Ordinance shall be in full force and effect 
immediately upon its passage.

Frank J. Bruzzese BY FAX AND REGULAR MAIL

Steubenville, OH 43952 


Re: Chapter 116, Codified Ordinances of the 
Village of Stratton 


Dear Mr. Bruzzese: 


Thank you for your letter of June 17, 1998, about the 
proposed modifications to Chapter 116 of Stratton’s 
ordinances. I was on assignment out of the country when it 
arrived and so was unable to respond until now. Please accept 
these comments simply as my observations, as I do not 
believe that we can negotiate the content of a municipal 
ordinance. If they are valid, perhaps you will consider them 
in your final draft. 


158a 
Appendix 1(5) 


At the outset, it seems to me that the proposed revised 
ordinance does not apply to the door-to-door ministry of 
Jehovah’s Witnesses. As we have previously written, 
Jehovah’s Witnesses call at residences to converse with the 
occupant about some topic of interest and about the Bible. 
In their door-to-door ministry, they do not canvass, solicit, 
peddle, hawk, or sell anything, and they do not make such 
calls for the purpose of advertising, promoting, selling, and/ 
or explaining any product, service, organization, or cause. 
Thus, if passed, we would consider the ordinance as not 
applying to Jehovah’s Witnesses, whether or not it otherwise 
would pass review on a constitutional basis. 


Your deletion from section 116.01 is good for free speech 
in Stratton. Adding the reference to the permit in that section 
is logical from a drafting standpoint. 


Some practical problems are inherent in section 
116.03(b)(S). Unless a complete listing of all residence 
addresses in the village is maintained at the Mayor’s office, 
people desiring permits will be forced to drive throughout 
the village to obtain addresses. For those desiring to engage 
in the regulated conduct at many or all residences in the village, 
this will present a logistical problem. It will also add to vehicular 
traffic on village streets. Will the statement ‘all residences in 
the village’ be deemed sufficient? It is not uncommon for 
residences to lack identifying addresses that are visible from 
the street. How should those be listed in order to comply 
with the ordinance? From a legal standpoint, I fail to see 
why it is necessary for an applicant to list each specific 
residence that he intends to visit (or why the permit issued 
under section 116.04 should be limited to such addresses). 


159a 
Appendix I(5) 


The proposed ordinance seems to indicate that the permit 
will be valid for the length of time stated on the registration 
form under section 116.03(b)(4). What if the registrant states 
that the length of time desired is ‘permanent’ or 
‘continuously’ or words to that effect? Will a permanent 
permit be issued? 


The federal courts have ruled that it is not legal to charge 
a fee for the ‘privilege’ of exercising first amendment rights 
(see, e.g., Murdock v. Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, 319 
U.S. 105, 63 S.Ct. 870 (1943)). The registration fee under 
section 116.04 is thus inappropriate. 


No federal appellate court case has upheld an ordinance 
limiting noncommercial first amendment activity to between 
9:00 a.m. and 5:00 p.m. Although the United States Supreme 
Court has not ruled on the issue, federal courts of appeals 
have ruled against municipal ordinances that prohibit such 
activity after 5:00 p.m. 


The addition of section 116.07 allows individual residents 
to give notice that they do not want uninvited solicitations. 
With this addition, it seems that the village does not need to 
otherwise regulate noncommercial speech activity. The 
ordinance could merely state that all persons described in 
section 116.01 must comply with section 116.07. If the village 
wants to further regulate commercial (including fund-raising) 
speech activity by requiring registration, payment of a fee, 
and a permit, it could do so. Such would be unnecessary for 
activity involving noncommercial speech. In other words, 
since residents would have a formal way of notifying 
unwanted visitors, there is no need to require registration by 
those conducting only noncommercial speech activity. 


160a 
Appendix 1(5) 


Even though we believe that neither the existing 
ordinance nor the proposed revised ordinance applies to the 
activities of Jehovah’s Witnesses, the local congregation 
would be willing to contact the Police Department in advance 
of going into the village, as was more fully discussed in my 
April 27, 1998, letter. If that is agreeable, then your client’s 
concerns should be resolved without regard to the wording 
of the ordinance. Of course, Jehovah’s Witnesses would abide 
by any posted sign clearly indicating that their visit is not 
welcome. 


Yours very truly, 


s/ Richard Moake 
Richard D. Moake - 


Associate General Counsel 
RDM:eln

ATTENTION: RICHARD D. MOAKE, Associate General 
Counsel (Legal Department) 


RE: CHAPTER 116, CODIFIED ORDINANCES OF 
THE VILLAGE OF STRATTON 


Dear Mr. Moake: 


After I received your July 2, 1998 letter containing 
comments on the draft modifications to Chapter 116, I 
incorporated some of your comments in my final draft, and 
presented the draft to Council, which passed Ordinance No. 
1998-5, which amended Ordinance No. 1996-06. 


163a 
Appendix 1I(5) 
A copy of the Ordinance is enclosed for your reference. 


In the final draft, I maintained the deletion from Section 
116.01, which you characterized as “good for free speech.” 
I added the reference to the permit in that section, as you 
thought would be “logical from a drafting standpoint.” 


With reference to your comment regarding “practical 
problems . . . inherent in Section 116.03(b)(5),” I concluded 
that a complete listing of all residence addresses in the Village 
would be available in the local directories and other records 
maintained at the Mayor’s office. I further concluded that 
Stratton is such a small town that people could, in fact, 
identify addresses without practical difficulty. (Remember, 
it is only the address to which you intend to solicit that must 
be listed on the application, therefore, it is not logical to 
take the position that physically locating that address is a 
practical or unreasonable burden.) 


With reference to the length of time, during which a 
permit will remain valid, a permit will be issued to any person 
who satisfies the prerequisites, provided by the Ordinance. 
There is no arbitrary cutoff, with reference to length of time. In 
answer to your question, if someone writes “permanent” or 
“continuously” or words to that effect, I conclude that the permit 
will remain in effect so long as that person continues to engage 
in the activity described in his application. Since the final 
draft deleted any charge of a fee for the permit, his applying 
for a new permit at a later time, as opposed to continuing his 
conduct under the same permit, will not raise a practical 
problem or legal problem for the permit holder or the Village 
officials, so long as the conduct continues unchanged. (Only a 
later failure to comply would be ground for revocation.) 


164a 
Appendix I(5) 


With reference to your comment in connection with 
Federal Court decisions which prohibit a fee for the 
“privilege” of exercising First Amendment Rights, I did not 
agree that the Registration Fee contained in the previous draft 
(previous draft section 116.04) was constitutionally infirm, 
however, the fee has been deleted. Therefore, anyone who 
complies with the Ordinance, will be entitled to a permit, 
free of charge. In fact, the final draft of the Ordinance does 
not leave discretion in the Mayor. Once the applicant 
complies with the prerequisites to a permit (all of which are 
objectively identifiable), the Mayor has no choice but to issue 
the permit, and issue it free of charge. 


The Ordinance maintains the 9:00 a.m.-5:00 p.m. 
reference. This is not a limit on First Amendment activity. 
Remember, the conduct which is addressed is the uninvited 
entry onto private property. 


With reference to your comment on Section 116.07 
(which allows individual residents to give notice that they 
do not permit uninvited entries), please note that the 
Ordinance makes no distinction between commercial versus 
noncommercial activity. It gives the private property owner/ 
occupier a way to communicate his own choice and document 
on arecord that will be available for inspection by the person 
who applies for a permit. The “No Solicitation Registration 
Forms” will be available at the office of the Mayor, so that 
the person who applies for a permit may see the property 
owners/occupiers prohibition, if any. This is a very fair way 
of notifying Village officials as to who is an unwanted visitor, 
and this is a very fair and practical way of notifying unwanted 
visitors, themselves. 


165a 
Appendix I(5) 


It seems to me that the solicitor’s duty to register and 
get a permit is a reasonable and constitutional requirement, 
which, among other things, identifies the people who go door- 
to-door and the nature and legitimacy of their purpose and 
organization, if any. It does not prohibit or limit any otherwise 
legal conduct; it merely requires registering in accordance 
with Section 116.03 and carrying/exhibiting the permit in 
accordance with Section 116.04. 


It is the owner/occupant who has the power to prohibit 
entry under this Ordinance, once the applicant has obtained 
his permit. 


In my opinion, the final draft protects all constitutional 
freedoms of any person who wants to solicit, etc., and, at the 
same time, protects the constitutional property rights of the 
private citizens, while not leaving overly broad discretion in 
the Village officials. 


I disagree with your opinion in connection with the 
applicability of the Ordinance. It applies to all organizations 
and persons equally. There is no distinction between 
commercial and noncommercial. There is no distinction or 
exception for church organizations or religious groups. 
Therefore, in my opinion, each individual who intends to 
engage in the conduct described in the Ordinance should 
register as required and should carry and exhibit the permit 
as required by the Ordinance. Such people should not contact 
the Police Department (as suggested in your July 2, 1998 
letter), but rather, they should come to the Mayor’s office. 


When the applicant appears at the Mayor’s office, he/ 
she will be able to see the registration forms of private 


166a 
Appendix 1(5) 


property owners, who prohibit entry, etc. In addition (and as 
suggested in your July 2, 1998 letter), we incorporated the 
idea of a sign posted on each of the private addresses where 
such entry is prohibited by the property owner. (Remember 

Section 116.07 requires the private property owner/occupier 
to do two (2) things — (1) post a “No Solicitation” sign on 
the property in a place visible to people who intend to enter 

and (2) file the “No Solicitation Registration Form” at the 
Mayor’s office. Thus, canvassers, solicitors, etc. have two 
(2) separate ways to identity the properties of citizens who 
have exercised their right to prohibit unauthorized entry. They 
can see the list (and the actual registration forms) at the 

Mayor’s office, and they can see the “No Solicitation” signs 

as they go from door-to-door. 


Thank you. 
Sincerely, 
s/ Frank J. Bruzzese 
FRANK J. BRU

with Section 116.07 of the Codified Ordinances of the Village 


of Stratton, Ohio:

APPENDIX 1(10) — DEFENDANTS’ EXHIBIT 39 
(DOCUMENTATION PROVIDED BY HELEN 
MAC MURRAY REGARDING CONSUMER FRAUD) 


TRANSIENT CRIME GROUPS 


FOR MANY YEARS, SOCIETIES HAVE BEEN PLAGUED 
BY VARIOUS BANDS OF ROVING THIEVES 
COMMITTING SCAMS. THESE CRIMINALS WERE 
INDISCRIMINATELY LABELED AS “GYPSIES”. SINCE 
THE TERM “GYPSY” CAN BE ASSOCIATED WITH 
ETHNIC GYPSIES KNOWN AS “ROM”, CAUTION IS 
NEEDED WHEN USING THAT TERM. THIS 
PUBLICATION WILL REFER TO THESE CRIMINALS 
GENERICALLY AS BEING “TRANSIENT CRIMINALS”. 
TRADITIONALLY, LAW ENFORCEMENT HAS 
TREATED THESE “TRANSIENT CRIMINALS” AND 
THEIR ACTIVITIES WITH LOW PRIORITY. THIS 
LIMITED ENFORCEMENT SERVED ONLY TO MOVE 
THE PROBLEM FROM ONE JURISDICTION TO 
ANOTHER. AS LAW ENFORCEMENT AGENCIES 
STARTED TO SHARE INFORMATION ON THE 
“TRANSIENT CRIMINALS”, IT BECAME APPARENT 
THAT THE PROBLEM WAS ACTUALLY A FORM OF 
NON-TRADITIONAL ORGANIZED CRIME. THIS WAS 
EVIDENT BY THE NETWORKING OF THE 
PARTICIPANTS, THE HIGH FINANCIAL LOSSES, AND 
THE LARGE NUMBER OF VICTIMS, OF WHICH MOST 
WERE ELDERLY. 


INVESTIGATIONS HAVE REVEALED THAT A LARGE 
PERCENTAGE OF THESE “TRANSIENT CRIMINALS” 
CAN BE LINKED TO THREE MAJOR “TRANSIENT 
GROUPS” — AMERICAN GYPSIES, EUROPEAN 


262a 
Appendix 1(10) 


GYPSIES, AND THE TRAVELERS. KNOWLEDGE OF 
THESE GROUPS AND THE “TRANSIENT CRIMINALS” 
WITHIN THEM CAN BE HELPFUL TO LAW 
ENFORCEMENT IN ITS SEARCH FOR CRIME 
SUSPECTS. THE SEARCH IS GENERALLY BASED ON 
THE TYPE OF CRIME AND THE DESCRIPTION OF THE 
SUSPECTS. 


THIS PUBLICATION PROVIDES A BRIEF DESCRIPTION 
OF THE “TRANSIENT GROUPS” AND THE 
“TRANSIENT CRIMINALS”. A LIST OF COMMON 
“TRANSIENT CRIMES”, SOME INVESTIGATIVE 
CONCERNS, AND A SOURCE FOR ASSISTANCE IN 
SUSPECT IDENTIFICATION IS ALSO PROVIDED. IT 
MUST BE UNDERSTOOD THAT THIS INFORMATION 
IS GENERAL IN NATURE, AND SHOULD NOT BE USED 
SOLELY AS A BASIS FOR ENFORCEMENT. 


PROFILES OF THE MAJOR “TRANSIENT GROUPS” 
1. AMERICAN GYPSIES 
2. EUROPEAN GYPSIES 
3. TRAVELERS 
* ENGLISH TRAVELERS 
+ IRISH TRAVELERS 


* SCOTTISH TRAVELERS 


263a 
Appendix 1(10) 


ETHNIC GYPSIES, ALSO KNOWN AS “ROM”, ARE 
BELIEVED TO HAVE ORIGINATED CENTURIES AGO 
IN THE PUNJAB REGION OF NORTHERN INDIA. 
MIGRATION TOOK THEM TO CENTRAL EUROPE BY 
THE 14™ CENTURY. BY THE 16™ CENTURY, THEY 
HAD SPREAD TO NORTHERN AND EASTERN EUROPE. 
MIGRATION TO THE UNITED STATES BEGAN IN THE 
LATE 19™ CENTURY. GYPSIES ARE NOW LABELED 
BASED ON THE TIME SPENT IN THE UNITED STATES. 
THE “AMERICAN GYPSIES” GENERALLY HAVE BEEN 
IN NORTH AMERICA FOR APPROXIMATELY 100 
YEARS, WHEREAS THE “EUROPEAN GYPSIES” FOR 
ONLY THE PAST 30 YEARS. 


SOME SCHOLARS SUGGEST THAT THE “TRAVELERS” 
ORIGINATED FROM THE “GYPSIES”. THIS IDEA IS 
BASED ON THE SIMILARITY OF THEIR “CLOSED 
SOCIETY” LIFESTYLE AND THEIR UNIQUE 
LANGUAGE (SLANG OR CANT). MOST “TRAVELERS” 
THOUGH DENY ANY ASSOCIATION WITH THE 
ETHNIC GYPSIES. THE “TRAVELER” GROUPS FIRST 
MIGRATED TO THE UNITED STATES, FROM EUROPE, 
DURING THE 18™ CENTURY, AND ARE LABELED BY 
THEIR COUNTRY OF ORIGIN. 


THE “TRANSIENT GROUPS” HAVE RETAINED 
VARIOUS CULTURAL PRACTICES AND SIMILARITIES, 
WHICH CAN BE DOCUMENTED. THESE SIMILARITIES 
ARE USEFUL WHEN LOCATING AND TRACKING 
THESE GROUPS. HOWEVER, THE “CRIMINAL 
ELEMENT” WITHIN THESE GROUPS HAVE 
DEMONSTRATED THE ABILITY TO ADAPT THEIR 


264a 
Appendix 1(10) 


LIFESTYLE AND CRIMINAL ACTIVITY WHEN 
NEEDED. CAUTION SHOULD BE USED IF TEMPTED 
TO DRAW ANY DEFINITIVE CONCLUSIONS ABOUT 
THE “TRANSIENT GROUPS” AND THE “TRANSIENT 
CRIMINALS”. 


COMMON CULTURAL DENOMINATORS 
* MAINTAIN A “CLOSED SOCIETY” BY LIMITING 
THEIR SOCIAL CONTACT WITH PERSONS OUTSIDE 
OF THEIR DISTINCT GROUP 


* MARRY WITHIN THEIR OWN GROUP, SOME 
MARRIAGES ARRANGED 


* DISTINCT LANGUAGE UNIQUE TO EACH GROUP 


* PATRIARCHAL FAMILY STRUCTURE (FATHER IN 
CONTROL) 


* LIMITED USE OF PUBLIC SCHOOLS 


ADDITIONAL COMMON DENOMINATORS 
FOR “CRIMINAL ELEMENT” 


* MANY INVESTIGATORS BELIEVE THAT THE 
“CRIMINAL ELEMENT” MAKE UP A LARGE 
PERCENTAGE OF THE RESPECTIVE GROUPS 


* MAINTAIN NOMADIC LIFESTYLE — WORLDWIDE 
NETWORK 


* TARGET THE ELDERLY 


265a 
Appendix 1(10) 
MASTERY OF NUMEROUS THEFT TYPE CRIMES 


MASTERY OF CONFIDENCE SCAMS, INSURANCE 
& CREDIT FRAUDS 


FEDERAL AND STATE TAX EVASION 


LOCAL BUSINESS LICENSES NOT OBTAINED, OR 
ARE FRAUDULENT 


PRACTICE HIGH PRESSURE SALES TACTICS 


MAKE EXTENSIVE USE OF ALIASES AND FALSE 
DOCUMENTS 


FREQUENT CASINOS AND RACE BETTING 
TRACKS 


CRIMINAL EXPERTISE PASSED DOWN AMONG 
FAMILY MEMBERS 


MAJOR “TRANSIENT GROUP” DESCRIPTIONS 
AMERICAN GYPSIES 


MIGRATED TO AMERICA AROUND 
Te euay ane GENERALLY SECOND OR THIRD 
GENERATION AMERICANS, BUT MOST STILL 
SPEAK IN ACCENTED ENGLISH AND ROMANESE 
(GYPSY LANGUAGE). 


THEIR PHYSICAL COMPLEXION CAN VARY FROM 
DARK TO LIGHT.

Appendix 1(10) 


* THE FEMALES OF THE “CRIMINAL ELEMENT” ARE 


GENERALLY KNOWN FOR THEIR INVOLVEMENT 
IN FORTUNE TELLING, SHOPLIFTING, AND 
INSURANCE FRAUDS. THE MALES ARE ACTIVE IN 
HOME REPAIR SCAMS, IMPOSTOR BURGLARIES, 


VEHICLE REPAIR/SALE SCAMS, AND METAL 
THEFTS. 


THE “CRIMINAL ELEMENT” CAN EASILY BE 
MISTAKEN FOR THE “CRIMINAL ELEMENT” 
WITHIN THE “ENGLISH TRAVELERS” BECAUSE OF 
THEIR INVOLVEMENT WITH SIMILAR TYPE 
FRAUDS AND HAVING SOME SIMILAR SURNAMES.

EUROPEAN GYPSIES 


* MOST ARE FROM EASTERN EUROPE, PRIMARILY 


FORMER IRON CURTAIN COUNTRIES (HUNGARY, 
POLAND, YUGOSLAVIA, ETC.) 


267a 
Appendix 1(10) 


* RELATIVELY NEW TO NORTH AMERICA (LAST 30 
YEARS) 


* SPEAK BROKEN ENGLISH OR COMBINATION OF 
ROMANESE (GYPSY LANGUAGE) AND VARIOUS 
EUROPEAN LANGUAGES 


* OFTEN DESCRIBED AS HISPANICS OR NATIVES OF 
INDIA 


* SHORTER THAN THE ANGLO POPULATION WITH 
DARK HAIR AND EYES 


* MANY WOMEN WEAR LONG SKIRTS WITH HAIR 
IN BUN OR BRAID 


* “CRIMINAL ELEMENT” GENERALLY GROUPED BY 
CRIME SPECIALTY: 


* HOME INVASION THEFTS 
* STORE DIVERSION THEFTS 


* MAKE USE OF “BOOSTER APRONS” TO CONCEAL 
STOLEN PROPERTY 


* STATUS WITH IMMIGRATION QUESTIONABLE 
HOME INVASION THEFTS 


THERE IS A GROUP WITHIN THE EUROPEAN 
GYPSIES THAT SPECIALIZES IN COMMITTING 
RESIDENTIAL OR “HOME INVASION” BURGLARIES. 
A LARGE PERCENTAGE OF THESE BURGLARS 


268a 
Appendix 1(10) 


APPEAR TO BE OF POLISH DESCENT. THEY ARE 
GENERALLY BASED IN NEW YORK, CHICAGO, AND 
OTTAWA, CANADA, WITH SMALLER GROUPS IN 
VIRGINIA, FLORIDA, AND NEW JERSEY. THESE 
CRIMINALS WILL TRAVEL IN FOUR DOOR PRIVATELY 
OWNED OR RENTAL VEHICLES, AND USUALLY 
WORK IN TEAMS OF THREE OR FOUR (MALE 
OPERATOR, FEMALE PASSENGERS). THE FEMALES 
WILL ENTER THE VICTIM’S RESIDENCE FOR THE 
PURPOSE OF STEALING MONEY, JEWELRY, OR 
SILVER. IF CONFRONTED, STATEMENTS SUCH AS 
“LOOKING FOR MY LOST DOG” OR “LOOKING FOR A 
PLACE TO RENT” ARE USED TO JUSTIFY ENTRY INTO 
THE RESIDENCE, AND TO DISTRACT HOMEOWNER. 


COMMON HOME INVASION SUSPECT SURNAMES: 


ANDRZEJ, BARBINSKI, BARCZEWSKA, 
BOGDANOWICZ, BRZEZINSKA, CIECIERSKA, 
DABROWSKA, DANIS, DEBOWSKA, DOLINSKA, 
DOMBROWSKI, DYTLOW, FORGAC, FYDRICH, 
GLOWACKA, GOWMAN, GRABOWSKA, HORVATH, 
JAWORSKA, KAMINSKA, KOLOMPAR, KOWALSKA, 
KWIATKOWSKI, LAKATOSZ, MAJEWSKA, 
MALINOWSKA, NOWAK, PACKOWSKA, 
PARCZEWSKA, PAWLOWSKA, PIHIK, 
PIENCZYKOWSKI, PLOWA, RAPACINSKA, SABO, 
SAK, STOBA, STOJKA, STRUPEK, TELESZ, 
WACKOWSKA, WLOCH, YAWORSKI, ZIELINSKI. 


(NAMES ENDING WITH “SKA” ARE FEMININE, “SKI” 
ARE MASCULINE) 


269a 
Appendix 1(10) 
STORE DIVERSION THEFTS 


ANOTHER CRIMINAL GROUP, WITHIN THE 
EUROPEAN GYPSIES, SPECIALIZES IN STORE 
DIVERSION THEFTS. A LARGE PERCENTAGE OF 
THESE CRIMINALS APPEAR TO BE OF YUGOSLAVIAN 
DESCENT, AND ARE MOSTLY BASED OUTSIDE OF 
CHICAGO, IN LINCOLNWOOD AND SKOKIE, ILLINOIS. 
THEY WILL TRAVEL THE COUNTRY IN LARGE 
AMERICAN MANUFACTURED VEHICLES WHICH ARE 
PERSONALLY OWNED OR RENTED. COMMERCIAL 
OR RETAIL BUSINESSES LOCATED NEAR A MAJOR 
HIGHWAY ARE GENERALLY TARGETED. A GROUP OF 
BOTH MALES AND FEMALES WILL ENTER THE 
BUSINESS AND ATTEMPT TO DISTRACT THE 
BUSINESS PERSONNEL. MONEY AND/OR PROPERTY 
IS THEN TAKEN FROM THE BUSINESS OFFICE OR 
SAFE. IF THE THEFT INITIALLY GOES UNNOTICED, 
IT MAY MISTAKENLY BE REPORTED AS AN 
“INTERNAL THEFT”, THEREFORE BRINGING 
SUSPICION ONTO THE BUSINESS EMPLOYEES. 


COMMON STORE DIVERSION SUSPECT SURNAMES: 


ARMANOV, BOGDANOV, DJENIKOV, DOBOBROV, 
GINISOV, IVANOV, JOSIPOVIC, KOMAROV, 
KONOVALOV, KONSTANTINOV, KOROTKOV, 
KUZNIKOV, MARTINOV, MORO, PAVLOV, PETROV, 
PETROVIC, RODIEKOV, SADRINOVIC, SAROV. 


270a 
Appendix I(10) 
TRAVELERS 


* FIRST CAME TO THE UNITED STATES IN LARGE 
NUMBERS DURING THE ENGLISH MIGRATION OF 
THE 1700’S, AND AGAIN DURING THE IRISH 
POTATO FAMINE IN THE 1840’S 


* HAVE TYPICALLY AMERICAN CAUCASIAN 
APPEARANCE, MOST WITH LIGHT COMPLEXION 


* MAY PUT THEIR CHILDREN IN PRIVATE SCHOOLS 


* USE TRAVEL TRAILER PARKS, MOBILE HOME 
PARKS, HOTELS, MOTELS, SHORT TERM 
APARTMENTS WITH RENTED FURNITURE, AND 
THE LOCAL ESTABLISHED TRAVELERS’ HOMES 


* THE MALE MEMBERS OF THE “CRIMINAL 
ELEMENT” ARE KNOWN FOR THEIR 
INVOLVEMENT IN HOME REPAIR SCAMS, 
IMPOSTOR BURGLARIES, SALE SCAMS, AND 
INSURANCE/CREDIT FRAUDS. THE FEMALE 
MEMBERS ENGAGE IN SHOPLIFTING, AND ASSIST 
THE MALES WITH THE INSURANCE AND CREDIT 
SCAMS. 


THE “TRAVELERS” ARE WELL INCORPORATED INTO 
MAINSTREAM SOCIETY. THEY HAVE FORMED 
VARIOUS BUSINESSES AND SERVICES THAT CAN BE 
USED TO LEGITIMIZE AND SUPPORT THOSE 
INVOLVED IN CRIMINAL ACTIVITIES.

Appendix 1(10) 
TRAILER PARK OWNERSHIP 
HOUSE TRAILER DISTRIBUTORS 


PAINT, “SEALER”, ASPHALT, AND TOOL 
DISTRIBUTORS

¢ PRINT OWN “RELIGIOUS MATERIAL” 
* PURPOSE: 
* PROVIDE MEETING PLACE (?) 
* TAX SHELTER (?)

* MAIL DROPS AND TELEPHONE MESSAGE 


SERVICES ARE USED TO DISTANCE THE 
CRIMINALS FROM THEIR ACTIVITIES 


“NON-TRAVELERS” MAY BE USED AS 
LABORERS, CHECK CASHING, CONTRACT 
COMPLETION, ETC., TO HINDER 
IDENTIFICATION OF CRIMINAL GROUP 


BUSINESSES CAN ACT AS CREDIT OR 
EMPLOYMENT VERIFICATION FOR “TRAVELERS” 
INVOLVED IN CREDIT SCAMS 


272a 
Appendix I(10) 
ENGLISH TRAVELERS 


THE ENGLISH TRAVELERS ARE BASED IN 
NUMEROUS LOCATIONS NATIONWIDE. THE 
“CRIMINAL ELEMENT” WITHIN THIS GROUP IS BEST 
KNOWN FOR ASPHALT PAVING SCAMS AND SEALING 
SCAMS. THEY ARE ALSO INVOLVED IN OTHER HOME 
REPAIR SCAMS, IMPOSTOR BURGLARIES, AND SALE 
SCAMS. THEY ARE EASILY MISTAKEN FOR THE 
“CRIMINAL ELEMENT” WITHIN THE “AMERICAN 
GYPSIES” BECAUSE OF THEIR INVOLVEMENT WITH 
SIMILAR TYPE FRAUDS AND HAVING SOME SIMILAR 
SURNAMES. 


COMMON ENGLISH TRAVELER SURNAMES: 


BAKER, BOSWELL, BREWER, BROADWAY, 
CARTER, COOPER, DAVIS, DILSAVER, HARRISON, 
HICKS, JOHNSTON, LOVELL, MULLEN, OAKS, 
RENNER, SARTIN, SMALL, SMITH, SPARROW, 
STANLEY, THOMPSON, WELLS, WILLIAMS, WRIGHT. 


IRISH TRAVELERS 


THE IRISH TRAVELERS ARE BASED IN VARIOUS 
LOCATIONS ACROSS THE UNITED STATES, THE MOST 
FAMOUS BEING A SECTION OF NORTH AUGUSTA, 
SOUTH CAROLINA, CALLED MURPHY VILLAGE. THE 
“CRIMINAL ELEMENT” WITHIN THIS GROUP IS BEST 
KNOWN FOR BARN OR HOUSE PAINTING SCAMS 
AND TOOL SALE SCAMS. THEY ARE ALSO INVOLVED 
IN ROOF COATING SCAMS AND LIGHTNING ROD 
SCAMS. 


273a 
Appendix 1(10) 
COMMON IRISH TRAVELER SURNAMES: 


BURKE, CARROLL, CORRIGAN, COSTELLO, 
DALEY, DONAHUE, DOUGHERTY, GORMAN, 
HANNAGAN, MACK, MCDONALD, MCNALLY, 
MULHOLLAND, NORMILE, O’HARA, RAFFERTY, 
RILEY, SHERLOCK, WHITE. 


SCOTTISH TRAVELERS 


THE SCOTTISH TRAVELERS ARE BASED IN 
NUMEROUS LOCATIONS NATIONWIDE. THE 
“CRIMINAL ELEMENT” WITHIN THIS GROUP IS BEST 
KNOWN FOR HOME REPAIR SCAMS AND SALE 
SCAMS. 


COMMON SCOTTISH TRAVELER SURNAMES: 


BISHOP, BURNS, CROLL, DAVIS, DOCHERTY, 
EVANS, FORREST, GORDON, HALLIDAY, HOLDEN,

SWEET, THOMPSON, TOWNSLEY, WATSON, WHITE,

“TRANSIENT CRIMINALS” WORK VEHICLE 


MANY “TRANSIENT CRIMES” GO UNNOTICED BY 
LAW ENFORCEMENT UNTIL A PATTERN IS 
IDENTIFIED THROUGH CRIME REPORTS. AE TIME 
DELAY BETWEEN THE CRIMINAL REPORT AND 
ANALYSIS, USUALLY RESULTS IN THE “TRANSIENT 


274a 
Appendix 1(10) 


CRIMINAL” HAVING MOVED ON TO OTHER 
JURISDICTIONS. RECOGNITION OF THE WORK 
VEHICLE WILL ALERT LAW ENFORCEMENT TO THE 
“TRANSIENT CRIMINAL’S” PRESENCE, HOPEFULLY 
BEFORE CRIMES ARE COMMITTED. 


THE WORK VEHICLES MAY BE OBSERVED DRIVING 
ON THE ROADWAY OR PARKED AT HOTELS, MOTELS, 
TRAVEL TRAILER PARKS, MOBILE HOME PARKS, 
SHORT TERM APARTMENTS, AND LOCAL 
ASSOCIATES’ HOMES. SOME BASIC VEHICLE 
INDICATORS: 


* MAGNETIC SIGNS DEPICTING VARIOUS 
BUSINESSES OR SERVICES 


* OUT-OF-STATE REGISTRATION PLATES, 
OR RECENTLY REGISTERED IN-STATE 
REGISTRATION PLATES 


* VEHICLES TITLED TO A FEMALE OR TOA 
PERSON NOT INVOLVED, WHICH HINDERS 
LAWFUL SEIZURE OF VEHICLE 


* RELIGIOUS PARAPHERNALIA SUCH AS 
BUMPER STICKERS, DASHBOARD STATUES, 
BIBLES, ETC. 


* AFTER MARKET WINDOW TINT (DARKER THAN 
FACTORY SPECS) 


* BOX TRAILERS USED TO CARRY PERSONAL 
POSSESSIONS 


275a 
Appendix 1(10)
