CONTRACTOR REGISTRATION PROGRAM 


I. HISTORY 


The need for a contractor registration process/disaster 
relief program first made itself apparent following the tornado 
that struck the city of Xenia is the mid-1970’s. Out-of-state 
and itinerate home improvement contractors flooded the area 
preying on consumers that were still in a state of shock. 
Literally hundreds of contractors received up-front moneys 
for clean-up and repairs and then disappeared without 
performing the agreed work. 


From an enforcement standpoint, attempting to locate 
the culprits was a nightmare. With no paperwork generated 
by the transactions, we were often left with only general 
physical descriptions of the contractors, or, in one case, “I 
think he was driving a white pickup truck.” Needless to say, 
we were not very successful in pursuing resolutions to the 
hundreds of consumer complaints that were filed. 


When the tornado hit the village of Cardington in the 
late 1970’s the attorney general’s office proposed the first 
contractor registration program to be implemented in Ohio. 


288a 
Appendix 1(10) 


After the village fathers had issued a resolution requiring 
contractors to register, the Consumer Protection Section 
administered the registration process on behalf of the local 
government. Localized damage, the small size of the 
community, and brief national media exposure may have been 
contributing factors, but the result was very few rip-offs. 
Those complaints that did result were more readily handled 
due to our ability to identify the contractors involved. 


In the late spring of 1985, a series of tornadoes struck a 
heavily populated area of northeastern Ohio and 
Pennsylvania. The cities of Niles and Newton Falls, as well 
as the unincorporated area of Liberty Township sustained 
millions of dollars worth of damage. Thousand of individual 
consumers were effected. The national media ran with the 
story and within days hundreds of contractors from as far 
away as Texas and Mississippi had poured into the area. 


The attorney general sent his investigators to propose 
and assist in implementing the registration procedure. The 
local officials, already aware of the need, met our proposal 
with open arms. Three separate registration centers were 
established within the 30 mile area and registration was 
started within 24 hours of our arrival. 


In the ensuing two week period over 600 contractors were 
registered. It has been estimated that at least that many more 
fled the area into Pennsylvania after learning of the mandate 
to be registered. 


The Consumer Protection Section received only two 
consumer complaints in the months following the disaster. 


289a 
Appendix 1I(10) 


One of the complainants sued the contractor and won a 
judgment for damages in the amount of $12,000.00. 


During the tornadoes in Southwestern Ohio (Harrison 
and Fairfield) and the flood in Shadyside investigators from 
the Consumer Protection Section were on the scene within 
hours and stayed until local officials were able to assume 
control of the registration process, a total of almost 30 days 
in the field. Consumer protection personnel continued to 
provide assistance with the verification process for weeks 
after departing the areas. Recent tornadoes in Montgomery 
City and Blue Ash prompted activation of a registration team. 
The team registered over 500 contractors in five days. Our 
attempts to register contractors in the aftermath of disasters 
have been tremendously successful. 


Il. PURPOSE AND GOALS 


Our primary purpose is to assist local government and 
its citizens in what could be their most difficult and 
demanding hour. We offer our expertise, manpower, and 
resources in an attempt to protect the consumer from potential 
damage and free local officials to tend to other matters. 


Another first priority goal is to better educate the 
consumer so that they may be better able to protect 
themselves. This is accomplished by way of peppering the 
effected area with flyers that outline consumer tips to be used 
when contracting for repairs, saturating the area with media 
announcements, and making Consumer Protection personnel 
available to the consumer at locations throughout the area. 


290a 
Appendix 1(10) 


Another goal is to make it inconvenient for the transient 
contractor to stay in the area. When he learns that he must 
register and wait for the verification to be completed it is 
our hope that the unscrupulous contractor will decide that it 
is not worth his time or effort to stay. 


If he does stay in the area, the registration will, hopefully, 
provide sufficient information so as to be able to locate the 
contractor in the event of a problem. 


Ill. REQUIREMENTS 


The Attorney General does not have the authority to order 
contractor registration, that power lies with local government. 


In a situation where the disaster strikes a city we suggest 
that building codes already on the books be amended to 
require registration prior to work beginning. We also suggest 
a criminal penalty for non-compliance, this will allow local 
law enforcemeist to remove the non-compliant contractor off 
the job and into custody. In villages or unincorporated areas 
of the county we recommend that the commissioners pass an 
emergency resolution requiring that contractors register. In 
any case we brief local law enforcement on the use of the 
Home Solicitation Sales Act, Ohio Revised Code 1345.21, 
to enable arrest on a misdemeanor charge. 


The Home Solicitation Sales Act (ORC 1345.21) 
specifies that if the supplier approaches the consumer and 
contracts for work at a price greater than $25.00 there must 
be a completed contract and a verbal and written three day 
right to cancel. Work cannot begin in the three days unless 


291a 
Appendix 1(10) 


the consumer provides a written, dated statement specifying 
the emergency and expressly waiving his right to cancel. 
Violation of this section is a minor misdemeanor. 


IV. WHAT WE DO 


At the request/mandate of local government we will 
administer the registration procedure. We will provide the 
manpower, forms, and resources needed to verify the 
information presented by the contractor. We will not approve 
or disapprove of any contractor. 


For example, if a contractor discloses that he is from 
out of state, has been found guilty of larceny by trick, and 
refuses to provide any other information, then, if his 
assertions are true, we would consider his registration 
verified. 


Once the contractor has submitted his Business 
Registration Form we will transport the information to 
Columbus and, when and if verified, return it to the disaster 
area. At this point the local officials can use the information 
to decide whether or not to issue permits, and consumers are 
urged to review the information before hiring the contractor.

Q. Mr. Ciranko, I would like to now turn your attention to 
the Watchtower Bible and Tract Society. Could you please 
explain to the Court how that functions? 


A. The Watchtower Bible and Tract Society is strictly a legal 
entity. It was formed for the purpose of holding title to 
property and being able to publish literature, things that the 
laws of the country required. 


The Watchtower Bible and Tract Society, just as its name 
indicates, it publishes the Watchtower magazine, the main 
journal of Jehovah’s Witnesses, it publishes Bibles as well 
as tracts and other publications that are used for Bible 
education the world over. 


The Watchtower Society also organizes conventions for 
Jehovah’s Witnesses. It also has a hand in the building of 
kingdom halls, our places of worship, and it cares for other 
matters that relate to the needs of the [18] congregations. 


Q. How does the Watchtower Bible and Tract Society, sir, 
disseminate the information it publishes? 


A. The materials that we publish are distributed through the 
congregations by Jehovah’s Witnesses, and I should say that 
all of Jehovah’s Witnesses participate in this work. 


313a 
Appendix J 


Q. When you say all of Jehovah’s Witnesses participate in 
this work, could you explain to the Court what you mean? 


A. Every one of Jehovah’s Witnesses who is a baptized 
member is considered a minister, and it is his privilege and 
his responsibility to preach the gospel of Christ. If I may 


have 4 Bible, I would cite a reference that gives us our 
commission to do so.

A. There is no charge for our literature. We leave all of our 
literature free of charge. 


Q. How is this work financed? 


A. It is funded by Jehovah’s Witnesses themselves, by our 
own members. Our organization is unique in that we do not 
tithe. We do not pass a collection plate. We do not impose 
duns or dues upon our members. We do not send out letters 
of solicitation. We do not solicit for money from house to 
house. 


We have contribution boxes set up at our kingdom halls, 
and anyone who wishes can put a donation there. No one 
knows what each one is donating; and if one cannot afford 
to donate, that’s fine. It’s done as a free will offering. 


Q. What provision would there then be for a householder, 
someone who is met in the public ministry, to donate to this 
work? 


A. Well, if an individual has an interest in doing that, we 
feel that it’s appropriate to advise them that they can certainly 
make a donation to Jehovah’s Witnesses for our worldwide 
work. However, that’s not the purpose in going [22] from 
house to house. Our purpose in going from house to house is 
to talk about the Bible, to offer a study of the Bible with 
people. Money is the least of our interests. 


Q. What then is the cost for the home Bible study? 


A. That is free # well. Our entire organization is of a 
charitable, nonprofit, tax-exempt nature. 


317a 
Appendix J 


Q. Could you explain to the Court how the local 
congregations function, Mr. Ciranko? 


A. The local congregation is patterned after what we read in 
the scriptures. The Bible tells us that Jesus Christ is the head 
of the congregation. So religiously Jehovah’s Witnesses are 
organized in a theocratic fashion, theocratic meaning ruled 
by the theos, God. We feel our organization is ruled by God. 
That’s where we receive our authority to preach, for instance. 
In the text that I read from Matthew 28, verse 19 and 20, in 
the verse just before that, verse 18, Jesus said, “All authority 
has been given me in heaven and on the earth,” and then in 
his next breath he said go therefore and preach. 


In the congregation we have what’s called 
elders, all of the spiritually qualified men in the caeetiine 
who meet the scripture outlined in the Bible for that office 
are appointed as elders. The elders take the lead in the 
ministry. They teach the congregation from the platform, and 
me help the members with their personal [23] problems as 
well. 


Q. You are using the expression “take the lead in the 
ministry.” What do you mean? 


A. Actually going from house to house. Also the 
congregation will meet on a regular basis. We conduct what 
is known as a meeting for field service where an elder will 
instruct the group for that period of activity, give some 
suggestions of things they might talk about when they find 
people at home and help to organize the work for that period. 


318a 
Appendix J 


Q. How is the preaching work organized within a particular 
community? How does that function? 


A. Every congregation has an assignment of territory, it could 
be an entire county, parts of counties, and they have definite 
boundaries assigned to them as to what area of that locality 


they are to preach in. 


Then once they have those boundaries, then locally an 
individual in a congregation, one of the elders or one of the

responsibility to take the entire territory and divide it into 
small workable areas and provide a map for that piece of 
territory. It’s actually produced as a map card to be given to 
the individual members of the congregation who will go out 
into that neighborhood to preach.

an individual who is adamant that they do not want Jehovah’s 
Witnesses to call on them any longer, I will endeavor to get 
their name, and | will certainly write that down as well as 
their address; and I will turn that in at the kingdom hall to 
the elder who is in charge of the territory. He will write up a 
note of that, put it inside the map card so that the next person 
who is assigned to work this territory will know not to call

A. Yes, at the discretion of the elders, when they feel it’s

To this day we still have a governing body of anointed 
Christian men who oversee the work of Jehovah’s Witnesses 
worldwide.

41Sa 
Appendix N

time which, I will refer to as the first generation of the 
ordinance that we have under discussion, came into play? 


A. Yes, sir. 


Q. Can you briefly explain to us what was the reasoning 
behind the initiation of the legislation for the Village of 
Stratton in 1996? 


A. The village council wanted me to draft legislation that 
would regulate the practice of soliciting, canvassing, 
hawking, peddling, so forth, in the territorial limits of the 
Village of Stratton, Ohio, based on recurring incidents and 
problems people had had over the years; and when I 
researched the problem in the village ordinances, I found 
just the old original ordinances that formed the village 
government from 1949. I felt they were woefully 
inadequately. So I redrafted an ordinance that became 
effective in 1996. 


Q. Can you briefly describe for us the information that was 
discussed with council concerning the initiation of the 1996 
legislation? 


416a 
Appendix N 


[138] A. Yes, sir. We discussed with council that the types 
of problems that had recurred over the years could in fact be 
regulated under an Ohio statute and under the Ohio 
Constitution. 


Q. Let’s stop right there. What problems are you making 
reference to? i‘ 


A. There had been problems with people going door to door 
at night in the late night hours, as late at 11:00 p.m., which 
caused some fear in some citizens, especially the older 
citizens or the people who lived alone. There had been 
incidents of people purporting to be legitimate salesmen who 
turned out to be less than legitimate. 


Q. Can you give us some examples of that, sir? 


A. Yes, sir. The type that I am sure you are familiar with, 
the aluminum siding, high-pressure salesmen, the people who 
purport to be in the construction business and take a down 
payment, representing that they are taking in advance the 
payment of money for materials and then never show up. 
There have been incidents where people, although their 
purpose may have been legitimate, made the homeowner feel 
that the homeowner couldn’t get rid of them, and they felt 
pressured. Things like that.

[139] A. My duties as solicitor?

village.

Moake sent me a letter on behalf of the Watchtower 
organization in New York, criticizing that ordinance and 
contending there were aspects of it that were unConstitutional 
under the First Amendment. 


So I began corresponding with Mr. Moake in order to 
address his concerns and correct any deficiencies in that 
ordinance that he would point out to me from the 
Constitutional aspect and try to arrive at an ordinance that 
was effective and Constitutional and satisfactory to Mr. 
Moake and his organization.

423a 
Appendix N 


Q. Did you have any telephone communication with Mr. 
Moake? 


A. If I did, I don’t recall any. 


Q. As aresult of these letters going back and forth between 
you and Mr. Moake, did you make changes to the 1996 
ordinance? 


[146] A. Yes, sir, I did. 
Q. What changes did you make? 


A. I made a variety of changes. First of all, from the outset 
I took Mr. Moake’s criticism and based on the research that 
I did starting with the case law he gave me, decided that if 
there is anything consistent in the Constitutional case law 
that I was reading, it is that the discretion in the village 
officials should be narrowed and reduced to the extent 
reasonable and at the same time leave the judgment and the 
decision making to the private property owner. As a matter 
of fact, that that would be the best possible regulatory scheme. 


So I created the concept of the owner’s registration form 
as a form of communication — well, as a form to be used by 
the owner to exercise his decision and to also communicate 
to the would be solicitors and canvassers. 


And then the other end of that was to reduce the amount 
of discretion that the village officials, including the mayor, 
would have, so that I would make the permit automatically 
triggered by a completed information form. 


424a 


Appendix N 


Mr. Moake also criticized — Essentially Mr. Moake was 
criticizing substituting the judgment of the officials for the 
citizens. So I created a way that the citizen would exercise 
his own judgment over his own private [147] property and 
reduce the amount of judgment that the officials could impose 
on either the would be solicitor or the citizen. 


I remember Mr. Moake also criticizing a $10 fee that we 
had for the registration, and I deleted the fee in the end, 
recognizing his point; and although I found support in the 
law for that fee, I just considered it unnecessary. So I deleted 
the fee. 


And I’m sure there are other things that are — I think 
I summarized my changes in my last letter to him. If I could 
just look at that. 


Q. Exhibit 24. 


A. Oh. One of his criticisms was he thought it would be 
burdensome for a person to have to actually walk around the 
town and look at all of the addresses and then write them by 
hand into his registration form when he was registering for a 
permit. 


Q. How do you answer that criticism? 


A. As I pointed out to him in my September 9 letter, the 
solicitor would not have to, with his own hand, write those 
addresses, nor would he have to walk around the town to 
look for all the addresses. Because Stratton is such a small 
town with only 70 houses, and the rest being trailers and 


425a 
Appendix N 


trailer courts, it was an easy matter for the village to create a 
list of residence addresses with a designation [148] telling 
you which ones are homes, which ones are businesses, and 
which ones are churches and so forth. 


And when the would be solicitor or canvasser goes in to 
register, he has a choice. If he only wants to go to a few 
“houses, he can write them in by hand. If he wants to go to a 
bunch of houses and he wants to take the time, he can write 
them in by hand, and we gave him space for that. But his 
other options would be to take the list that the clerk makes 
available and check off from that list the ones he wants to go 
to; or if he wants to go to them all, write in on the blank of 
the form, I want to go to all the ones on the attached list, and 
attach the list. 


Q. So it’s incorporated by reference? 

A. Yes, and it’s a tool that the owner can use. 

Q. How is this different from a phone book? 

A. Itisn’t. The information on the list that we make available 
to the registrant is in the phone book and it’s also in the 
directories, or sometimes are called the crisscross directory. 
Q. Let’s stop at this point. What is the crisscross? 

A. To me it’s like a phone book, but you look up the street 
and address, and it gives the name of the person; whereas 


the phone book, you look up the name and then it gives the 
street, the address and the phone number.

this straight. When the little pig-tailed, freckle-faced girl

442a 


Appendix N 


THE COURT: As far as the language, though, 
canvassing really isn’t one of the verbs they’ re using. It talks 
about people who are canvassers, solicitors, peddlers, 
hawkers, itinerant merchants or transient vendors. Then it 
says that they’re in town for the purpose of advertising, 
promoting, selling and/or explaining. 


There are really three components. One is who these 
people are in terms of what they are doing. That’s the 
canvassing — whether they’re canvassers, solicitors, 
peddlers, etcetera. The second part is the action verb, [170] 
promoting, selling and/or explaining; and the third is a 
product, service, organization or cause. | view this in three 
parts. I think the language of the ordinance is clearly in three 
parts.

Q. Using our hypothetical about the individual who merely

falls within the ambit of the statute?

solicitor and he’s also a canvasser if he’s going from place 
to place. 


Q. How is that so, sir? 


443a 
Appendix N 


A. Because the plain meaning of the word “canvasser” is a 
person who goes from place to place for discussion. I am 
pretty sure that’s also the dictionary definition, and I think 
it’s the case law definition. The plain meaning of the word 
“solicitor” is a person who, whether he does it from place to 
place or in a single location, appeals to another person for 
some goal or for some thing; and I am very sure that I recall 
case law that says, when you’re going for a religious purpose, 
you are a solicitor. 


Q. I am sorry, sir, I didn’t mean to cut you off. 


[171JA. Then the second part, as His Honor breaks it down, 
is this person is explaining; and then the third part is what 
he is explaining, if it’s a religious cause, we have got all 
three parts that put him within the ambit of this ordinance. 


Q. I appreciate your answer, sir. What I am asking you 
specifically, an individual who just wants to share one Bible 
thought, how is that a cause? 


A. A cause is a principle or a movement to include religious 
principles, to include religious movements, that is certainly 
the plain meaning of the word. That is certainly the dictionary 
meaning of the word. So when this person is preaching the 
Bible or this passage, he is promoting a religious cause.

BY MR. POLIDORO: 


Q. Mr. Bruzzese, in your role as village solicitor, is it your 
opinion that there is a Constitutional right not to be annoyed 
in one’s own home? 


A. In my opinion there is a Constitutional right to be free 
from any state action that assists an intrusion into your home. 
Constitutional right, as I said in my deposition, protects you 
from government. It doesn’t protect you from a private citizen 
independently annoying you as your common law and 
statutory rights do protect you. 


447a 
Appendix N 


Q. So it’s your recollection in your deposition you did testify 
that it’s your opinion that one has a Constitutional right not 
to be annoyed in their own home, correct? 


A. My recollection is in my deposition I said one of the 
homeowner’s right that | considered was a Constitutional 
right to property and a Constitutional right to privacy and 
also his common law rights of privacy and rights against 
trespass; and somebody, and I can’t remember if it was you 
or Ms. Gellman, pursued the point on whether I believed the 
Constitution protected the homeowner against a private 
individual alone making these intrusions, and I think I said 
no, but that the village as a government couldn’t have [177] 
a state action that assists him and pushes him or helps him 
go into your home because that’s what the Constitution 
protects him against. 


We couldn’t give you a permit that says, you are allowed 
in my home no matter what I say. That would be a state action 
forcing me to let you into my home, and my Constitutional 
right to property and privacy would protect me against that. 


Q. Thank you.

deposition?

Q. Sir, would you not agree that a no trespassing sign on a 
home in the Village of Stratton would serve to keep away 
unwanted door-to-door visitors? 


A. I would certainly not agree with that. 
Q. Why not, sir? 
A. No trespassing signs have — Signs have failed to keep

include our signs at the north aid south ends of the village. 


Number two, our system is more specific. When you look 
at a no trespassing sign and you are going to go door to door 
selling eggs, you think, well, I am not trespassing. | am an 


45la 


Appendix N 


egg salesman. But our system is better than a no trespassing 
sign. It says specifically, I, the property owner, don’t want 
any salesmen coming to my door. So you get better 
information from that. 


Number three, the no trespassing sign to me is one size 
fits all, and my experience has been that the property owner 
really doesn’t mean that the pig-tailed girl scout cookie 
person is a trespasser when she comes to the door; whereas 
maybe the would be thief is. 


But our ordinance lets the property owner clearly specify, 
in a way that’s communicated to people, who is allowed to 
come on to my private property and who is not, [182] and it 
does that worlds better than a no trespassing sign.

Q. Let me ask you, sir, how does applying for a permit help

Q. But — 


A. We don’t have to argue later what he said. When there is 
an elderly, 86-year-old lady in court, and we’re arguing what 
did he say to her or what did he not say, how did he represent 
himself, it’s her against him. But we have an additional, at 
least one additional witness, the clerk who saw the guy. We 
have written evidence of what he represented himself to be. 
So when he says, I’m with the American Youth Organization, 
yaat’s my affiliation, and he has to show some credentials, 
we have got something to go on to prove that you are not 
with that organization or there is no such organization, and, 
therefore, you may be a fraud. 


Whereas this 86-year-old lady is going to have to 
remember, what organization did you say that you were 
affiliated with? What really did you promise to do? And you 
are going to be there, if we ever catch you, saying, I never 


45Sa 
Appendix N 


promised I was going to replace three shingles, and her roof 
was going to be fine for $10. 


Yes, the homeowner is a witness. The more witnesses, 
the merrier; the more documentary evidence and identifying

enforcement. I don’t think anybody can argue with that. So I 
like the registration and the owner’s [186] registration better 
than just the no trespassing sign.

an alleged fraud. Yes, sir, I can. 
Q. In door-to-door solicitation?

464a 


Appendix O 


A. There’s a multitude of different products that we see out

Q. Based on your knowledge and experience as a section 
chief, is there a particular group or groups of people who 
may be more susceptible to consumer fraud and other 
consumer types of problems? 


A. In my experience we have noticed that today’s scam artists 
tend to focus on the most vulnerable populations, and we 
find those to be senior citizens and people with low education 
and low income levels. 


Q. When you talk about senior citizens or the elderly, could 
you give us an idea age-wise what you are talking about? 


A. Well, we don’t really have a set age range. I think that 
maybe 50 and over is a general assumption. However, we 
also take into consideration when individuals may be [213] 
disabled or may have other things that make it more difficult 
for them to be able to enter into consumer transactions. 


Q. What you just said about the elderly and/or senior citizens, 
does that hold true for door-to-door solicitations as well? 


A. Most certainly. Because in fact senior citizens are the ones 
who are typically home nine to five. So I think in fact a higher 


465a 


Appendix O 


percentage of them fall victim to door-to-door kinds of 
solicitation because they are there. Same with telemarketing 
fraud, they are there to answer the phone.

section has taken in the door to door, other areas that form 
the basis of your view on the elderly being particularly 
susceptible? 


A. Just in regards to the cases that we see, especially the 
ones that are most egregious, they almost entirely seem to 
be senior citizens. In my travels throughout the country, in 
participating in seminars, in listening to other individuals 
speak, again what seems to be prevalent throughout the 
United States with the door-to-door solicitations that the 
intimidation factor again seems to make the senior more 
vulnerable; and because they are more [214] vulnerable, they 
seem to be attacked more often. So they seem to be targets. 


Q. Is there a difference there between door to door and 
telemarketing from that standpoint? 


A. Actually they’re similar. However, as much as I think 
seniors do have a hard time hanging up the phone, a lot of 
times on the door-to-door solicitation, in fact one of the 
reasons the Home Sales Solicitation Act was implemented 
was because once these people are in your home, we still 
hear stories about them staying there three, four hours, and 
senior citizens being unable — being intimidated by the 
individual, and they don’t want to be rude, and they have a 
hard time getting the person to leave.

protection section, do you have an opinion whether an

helpful in combatting consumer fraud and related consumer 
problems in door-to-door solicitations? 


A. Yes, I have an opinion. 

Q. What is that opinion? 

A. My opinion is it would indeed be helpful. 

Q. Why would that be helpful? 

A. Really for a variety of reasons. First is just preventive 
medicine. We spend a lot of time on consumer education 
trying to get the word out. Unfortunately, that always doesn’t 


work. So if there were a registration requirement in a 
community, it’s our experience that fraudulent, door-to-door 


468a 


Appendix O 


solicitors will pass that community by. So in one sense it 
may avoid trouble before it starts. 


Additionally, having the information, the solicitor’s name 
and the additional things that are provided by this ordinance, 
again if a scam or fraud does occur, then the law enforcement 
or the investigators in my office would be assisted by that 
information. 


Also one of the main things that seniors tell me [217] is 
that they can’t tell who the fraudulent solicitor is from a good 
guy, and that’s for a very good reason. Because con artists 
are very slick and very savvy and very nice, and the senior 
has a hard time telling. So if a permit was required, then the 
consumer would be able to know, one, if they had to sign up, 
if someone was at the door, they are going to know that they 
are dealing with someone fraudulent because they are 
disobeying the law; and, two, the fact they have a permit in 
their hand, the consumer who doesn’t have a sign can ask to 
see the permit; and if they didn’t have a permit, they will 
have a better idea of knowing who — this person is again 
violating the law, and they will have an easier time shutting 
the door. 


Q. Very good. Would you say that door-to-door solicitations, 
do they still cause consumer harm throughout Ohio? 


A. Very much so, yes. 
Q. In what ways? 


A. Oh, well, in a variety of ways. Right now our main priority 
in our section is home improvement fraud, and we find that 


469a 
Appendix O 


a significant amount of home improvement work is solicited 
door to door. In fact one of the more fraudulent groups out 
there are called the travelers or the gypsies; and in fact this 
group comes up to Ohio — In fact I just got a call from the 
Toledo area today — but they come up [218] to Ohio and 
various states, and they just sweep through communities, and 
they are — What they will do is knock on your door and say, 
we notice that the shingles on your roof are coming off or 
the blacktop needs work to be done; and what they will do is 
agree to do it for the consumer for a very inexpensive price, 
and they will perform the work, the blacktop is typically just 
a black paint that washes off, the roof shingles will fall back 
off, and the paint will wash off your house; and then 
frequently what they will do, the $200 paint job that you 
agreed to in the morning will be an $800 job by the end of 
the afternoon. 


And they can be very intimidating. They focus primarily 
on communities just as you have described Stratton to be, 
and again they also focus on senior citizens, and they look 
for neighborhoods with the well-kept lawns and the little 
lawn ornaments and things that to them say they are senior 
citizens.

Q. Olay. Very good. In your experience as section chief have

the local government level for would be solicitors? 


A. Yes, I have. 
[222] Q. And what type of setup is that? 


A. Well, I’m aware that many municipalities have these type 
of ordinances. Specifically, | am familiar with ordinances 
that are enacted after national disasters when the Attorney 
General’s disaster program has taken effect. 


Q. First of all, what is the disaster program that your office 
has? 


A. The disaster relief program was implemented after the 
Xenia tornado. Because of the amount of national press that 
the terrible tornado received, literally thousands of 
contractors came to the Xenia area from all over the country. 
Unfortunately what happened was the tornado victims were 
secondly victimized again by folks coming to rebuild the 
house and taking the insurance check and either doing 
slipshod work or taking the check and leaving; and we had 
thousands of complaints filed with our office afterwards and 
basically could do nothing because we couldn’t find these 
people. We didn’t know where they were. It was just 
impossible. 


So after that we implemented a computer program, and 
after a disaster we offered to municipalities a computerized 


474a 
Appendix O 


program that goes hand in hand with a contractor registration 
requirement which they pass by ordinance. The ordinance 
requires for a certain time period all contractors to register 
and to provide [223] significant background information 
which then is plugged in our computer system and which we 
verify. It includes a criminal background check. It includes 
past bankruptcies, past action in consumer fraud. It’s really 
very thorough, and it can be very helpful in having the bad 
guys to stay away to begin with; or if we do have a contractor 
who registers and there is a problem, we have a sufficient 
amount of information on him to go back on him. 


In fact most recently we used it after the Ciacinnati 
tornado earlier, and again we had hundreds of contractors, 
and it was amazing how many had criminal backgrounds that 
we would not have found out about but for this type of 
registration program. 


Q. So would you say there are several types of benefit that 
can come from some type of ordinance that would require 
registration of would be solicitors? 


A. Right. Like I said, actually similar to my answer about 
the Stratton ordinance, one, it prevents a lot of times the bad 
guying coming into town to begin with because they’re afraid 
of registration; and secondly, it provides law enforcement 
and our office with some information to go after the 
individuals who may have committed a fraud.

name and information can be used back and forth. So you

try to give consumers, especially senior citizens, a bright line. 
When there is a brigit line as to what is the law and what is 
not the law, and somebody is not obeying the law, again they 
are going to have a better idea who is [236] legitimate and 
who is not. 


The second goes to behavioral concerns. Unfortunately, 
we find certain individuals, again many of them senior 
citizens, when they are in the habit of talking on the phone 
or answering the door to solicitors, that they become 
comfortable with this interchange; and unfortunately, that 
type of person is going to be most prone to the fraudulent 
either telemarketer or person at the door because they are 
used to engaging in those types of behaviors. 


So, for example, when we have a particularly bad 
problem with telemarketing fraud, our number one 
recommendation is to disconnect the phone and get a new 
phone number. Unfortunately, you can’t get a new home every 
time you have problems with door-to-door solicitations. So 
sometimes extra measures are necessary.

[243] REDIRECT EXAMINATION 


BY MS. BREY: 


Q. You were asked a few moments ago on cross-examination 
about whether there are certain statistics on consumer fraud. 
Ms. MacMurray, do you need statistics to render an opinion 
whether an ordinance, such as the Stratton ordinance here, 
would be helpful to combat consumer fraud? 


49la 
Appendix O 


A. No, I don’t need statistics. I need to go to Dayton and 
view a woman’s home that has been demolished and the hole 
that’s been left when the contractor takes off. I mean I see 
that type of harm on a regular basis, and I hear about it on a 
national basis. When you see that happen to even one 
80-year-old woman, you don’t need statistics to tell you that 
it’s a huge problem. 


Q. In this case you mentioned about the 80-year-old woman 
in Dayton. Would you tell us a little bit more? 


A. Yes. She had a fire to her home, and a door-to-door home 
improvement contractor approached her at her front door and 
said that the home needs to be demolished, and I will take 
care of it. So she took her $40,000 insurance check and gave 
it to this individual. He came back, dug the foundation and 
was never heard or seen from again. 


The consumer’s home was then put in Court order 
because she had created a public nuisance, and they were 
threatening to put her in jail. She comes to my office [244] 
with this sad story, and we were able to hunt this fellow down, 
and we did obtain an injunction against him, and he was 
forced to come in, and he’s being made to do the work and 
working on paying the woman back her money. 


Q. You were asked also, I believe, about whether you were 
retained for the purpose of testifying today. 


A. Yes. 


Q. I want to clarify for the record, were you paid a fee for 
coming today? 


492a 


Appendix O 


A. No, no. Under the Consumer Sales Practice Act we are 


charged statutorily with conducting consumer education and 
outreach. So that’s what I do. 


Q. So you’re here with the Attorney General’s permission 
today? 


A. That’s correct. 


Q. When you talk about, just now you mentioned about the 
outreach and education. I think you also said that’s helpful 
in dealing with, talking with the elderly and other folks about 
the consumer problems that are out there, is that correct? 


A. Again prevention is a major key. In fact we are turning 
more and more nationally away from the trend of doing the 
legal enforcement actions and trying to put more dollars into 


consumer education to try to prevent the harm before it 
occurs.
